ADAMS–MÖBIUS PRIMITIVE INVERSION AND
FROBENIUS-CLASS PRODUCT CONSTANTS FOR
FINITE-GROUP EXTENSIONS OF SHIFTS OF FINITE
TYPE
HAOBO MA
Abstract. For a mixing subshift of finite type carrying a finite-group
cocycle, we study how finite-dimensional twisted determinants determine primitive orbit data by conjugacy class. Periodic labels record
powers of primitive labels, so passing from periodic determinant logarithms to primitive label series requires Adams operations g 7→ g m
together with Möbius inversion. This yields an explicit Peter–Weyl determinant formula for the primitive channels. Under the strict twisted
spectral gap η < λ, the channels have Perron-point values and give
class-indexed Mertens constants. For Euler products we keep the classical Frobenius-class existence and exponent |C|/|G|, but identify the remaining constant by a branch-compatible, Adams-corrected fixed-label
determinant formula: the scalar Perron channel is separated, the boundary triple (1, 1, 1) is deleted, and the non-trivial coordinates are Fϱ , not
the periodic Artin logarithms Lϱ . We prove determinant rigidity for
these constants and give a local finite S3 witness, with explicit values
Fε (1/2) and Fρ (1/2), showing that naive Artin substitution fails at the
product-constant level. Scalar finite parts and the canonical cyclic-lift
root layer serve only as finite-matrix normalisation and comparison devices; the main result is the Adams-corrected symbolic-dynamical determinant formula for primitive Frobenius-class constants.

1. Introduction
The problem addressed in this paper is primitive orbit determinant read+
out for finite-group extensions of mixing subshifts of finite type. Let (XA
, σ)
be a mixing subshift with primitive adjacency matrix A, let λ = ρ(A), and
let τ : EA → G be a cocycle into a finite group. For every irreducible representation ϱ of G, the twisted transition matrix Aϱ records periodic labels
through its traces. These traces are periodic data. The primitive labels
are subtler: when a primitive orbit with label g is repeated m times, its
label becomes g m . Thus primitive extraction is not ordinary Möbius inversion in the non-abelian setting; it must be coupled to the Adams operations
ψ m ϕ(g) = ϕ(g m ) on class functions.
The centre of the paper is the Adams–Möbius passage from twisted dynamical determinants to primitive Peter–Weyl channels and then to the
constants in Frobenius-class Euler products. The classical Frobenius-class

Mertens asymptotic supplies the exponent |C|/|G|:
PC (N ) = KC N −|C|/|G| (1 + o(1)).
The finite-state problem addressed here is the determinant computation and
rigidity of the constant KC after that classical asymptotic layer has been
separated. Under the strict twisted gap its non-trivial Fourier coordinates
are given by the fixed-label Euler transform

in the finite S3 model of Appendix A. The contribution is therefore the
Adams-corrected fixed-label determinant formula for KC , its finite recovery,
and the obstruction to Artin substitution, after the classical class-Mertens
exponent has been separated.
The finite-group argument is organized around three main theorems.
Main Theorem I is the Adams–Möbius inversion theorem: twisted determinant logarithms determine the primitive Peter–Weyl channels. Main
Theorem II is the Perron-point theorem: after the finite-state gap η < λ is
imposed, those primitive channels evaluate at z = λ−1 and give the additive
conjugacy-class Mertens constants. Main Theorem III is the product
theorem: the same data, now through the fixed-label Euler transform Fϱ ,
give the determinant readout of the positive Frobenius-class Euler-product
constants, while the existence of the product asymptotic and its exponent
remain part of the classical comparison layer. The other sections record
quotient and shadow functoriality, supply the trivial-channel normalisation,
give finite checks for the gap hypothesis, and provide exact rational enclosures for the local S3 example and the Main Theorem III evaluation. The
class transform used throughout is the Hermitian character-orthogonality
convention: its forward coefficient is χ∗ϱ (C) := χϱ (C), equivalently χϱ (C −1 )
for unitary characters, while inverse readout of a non-trivial Fourier
coordinate uses χϱ (C) without the star.
The scalar finite part of ζA and the cyclic root-of-unity layer are used in
two specific ways. The finite part supplies the trivial-channel normalisation
M(A), while root-of-unity sampling is attached to the canonical cyclic lift
in which every edge is labelled by the generator of Z/qZ, rather than to
arbitrary cyclic quotients of the fixed cocycle. The logical direction of the
proofs is

The proof chain is short: determinant traces give periodic Peter–Weyl
channels; Adams–Möbius inversion extracts fixed primitive labels; the strict
twisted gap permits evaluation at z = λ−1 ; and character orthogonality
converts those values into class Mertens and class-product constants. The
later quotient, eta-gap, scalar, and finite-enclosure sections serve this chain
by checking hypotheses or computing its ingredients in finite models. The
scalar dynamical ζ-function

These citations give context only; the proofs below use the fixed finite matrices and the stated Perron-point gap.

For a conjugacy class C ⊂ G, let pn,C denote the number of primitive orbits

1.2. Contributions and scope. The paper solves the following finite-state
problem. For a fixed matrix extension (A, τ, G), determine from the twisted
determinant family {det(I − zAϱ ) : ϱ ∈ Irr(G)} the primitive Peter–Weyl
coordinates at the Perron point and hence the constants in conjugacy-class
Mertens sums and Frobenius-class Euler products.
The main results are organized as follows.
(i) Main Theorem I gives the finite Adams–Möbius inversion formula that
recovers the primitive series Πϕ (z) from the determinant logarithms
on |z| < λ−1 . The Adams operation ψ m ϕ(g) = ϕ(g m ) is forced by the
repetition rule for primitive labels.
(ii) Main Theorem II assumes the strict twisted gap η < λ and evaluates
the non-trivial primitive channels at z = λ−1 , giving the Perron-point
class-Mertens constants by character orthogonality.
(iii) Main Theorem III proves the Adams-corrected determinant formula
for the Frobenius-class product constant. The standard Frobeniusclass product theorem supplies the existence of the product asymptotic
and the exponent |C|/|G|; the finite-state argument here identifies

Proposition 1.1 (Class-product calibration for Main Theorem III). Under the hypotheses of Main Theorem III, the Frobenius-class product asymptotic splits into two logically separate layers. The existence of the product
asymptotic and the exponent |C|/|G| are the classical Chebotarev–Mertens
input, while the constant KC is the finite-state object proved in this paper.
More precisely, after the scalar finite-part normalisation C(A) is fixed, the
non-trivial contribution to every KC is exactly the Hermitian class Fourier
transform of the fixed-label Euler vector {Fϱ (λ−1 )}ϱ̸=1 , with

by the periodic Artin logarithm vector {Lϱ (λ−1 )}ϱ̸=1 gives the same classproduct constants for all conjugacy classes if and only if the Peter–Weyl
Adams-obstruction vector vanishes.

Finally, Proposition 3.40 separates the fixed-label Euler transform from the
periodic Artin logarithm, and Proposition 3.55 and theorem 3.72 prove that
the class-product constants obtained by Artin substitution agree with the

Peter–Weyl obstruction vector Fϱ (λ−1 ) − Lϱ (λ−1 ) has zero class Fourier
transform, equivalently when all its non-trivial Peter–Weyl coordinates vanish. This proves the stated calibration.

The remaining sections supply the normalisations and examples needed
for this chain. Scalar finite parts fix the trivial-channel normalization; quotient and shadow arguments identify the coordinates retained by abelian
shadows; eta-gap tests give finite checks for the Perron-point hypothesis;
and the S3 computation gives a concrete Artin-substitution obstruction. For
a fixed extension (A, τ, G), the central formulas use only the matrices Aϱ ,
the character table, the Adams coefficients for the power maps g 7→ g m , the
Perron data (λ, η), and the scalar constant M(A). The trace identity and
Adams–Möbius extraction give Main Theorem I; the strict gap permits the
Perron-point evaluations of Main Theorem II; and the product-tail together
with the fixed-label determinant calculation gives Main Theorem III.

The inputs for Main Theorems I–III are the finite adjacency matrix A, the
finite group G, the twisted matrices Aϱ , the character table and Adams coefficients of G, the Perron root λ, and, for the Perron-point statements, the
strict gap η < λ. Scalar normalisation enters the product formula through
C(A) and M(A) = γ + FP(A), proved later from the scalar determinant
in Section 6. Main Theorem I is assembled in Proposition 3.1; Main Theorem II is Theorems 3.33 and 3.79; and Main Theorem III is Theorems 3.42
and 3.47 and proposition 3.64. In Main Theorem III every determinant logarithm reaches KC through the fixed-label coordinates Fϱ (λ−1 ), ϱ ̸= 1. The
(1)
scalar Perron triple (r, m, π) = (1, 1, 1) has coefficient aϱ,1 = 0 in every nontrivial coordinate, by Lemma 3.63; hence it is removed before the boundary
determinant evaluation in Propositions 3.64 and 3.67.
All three main theorems are finite-state statements. Main Theorem I uses
the finite twisted trace identity, determinant logarithms, Adams–Möbius
inversion, and Peter–Weyl extraction. Main Theorem II adds the strict
finite-dimensional gap η < λ in order to take Perron-point values. Main
Theorem III then combines the resulting additive class constants with the
fixed-label Euler transform and the branch-compatible determinant evaluation of Fϱ (λ−1 ). Thus the proof uses only finite matrices, finite-group
character operations, scalar finite parts, and Perron–Frobenius theory for
finite non-negative matrices.
Under the hypotheses of Main Theorem III the class product has the form
PC (N ) = KC N −|C|/|G| (1 + O(N −1 ))
for every conjugacy class C ⊂ G. The exponent |C|/|G| is the Frobeniusdensity term inherited from the classical orbit-counting theory. The finitestate contribution of this paper is the non-scalar constant coordinate

The scalar finite part of ζA , the canonical cyclic-lift comparison calculation, and the rational interval checks in the appendices play auxiliary roles.
The scalar calculation supplies the trivial-channel value M(A); the cyclic
lift supplies a fixed-matrix comparison model; and the interval checks make
the finite S3 witness reproducible. The theorem chain does not infer inverse
reconstruction from these auxiliary devices. The lower-order-tail stability
lemma in Proposition 3.70 shows that compensated lower-order tails vanish
in the constants used by Main Theorems II–III.
The three statements below summarize the main results in the order used
in the paper. Main Theorem I recovers primitive Peter–Weyl channels from

Euler-product constant.

The factor |C|/|G| multiplies the entire Hermitian class-indicator bracket,
including the non-trivial fixed-label sum. The product existence, density, and
exponent are the classical Frobenius-class Mertens layer. The formula for the
constant is obtained in this paper from the additive class Mertens theorem,
the absolutely convergent r ≥ 2 product tail, finite character orthogonality,
and the determinant evaluation of the fixed-label values Fϱ (λ−1 ). Each such
value is computed from the same finite twisted determinant data as Main
Theorem I by substituting the Adams–Möbius determinant formula into the
displayed series. This gives the primitive-label Euler determinant formula
for KC and the obstruction to replacing Fϱ by the periodic Artin logarithm.
Moreover Corollary 3.57 shows that, after the classical exponent and scalar
normalisation are fixed, no other branch-compatible non-trivial Peter–Weyl
vector yields the same class-product constants. The determinant evaluation
uses the fixed branch convention at λ−1 ; the scalar Perron triple (1, 1, 1) is
deleted before the non-trivial values Fϱ (λ−1 ) are evaluated.
The proof is Theorems 3.11 and 3.42, with determinant recovery supplied
by Proposition 3.64, theorem 3.47, and corollary 3.49. The comparison with
the periodic Artin logarithm is Proposition 3.55 and theorem 3.72; the finite
S3 rational bounds later give a concrete example, not a hypothesis of the
theorem.
After the main product formula is proved, two short consequences record
its stability and coordinate meaning: lower-order finite tails with zero Abel
contribution do not change MC (A, τ ; G), KC , or Fϱ (λ−1 ), and the fixed
branch-compatible determinant chart has the expected non-trivial Peter–
Weyl coordinate rank. These are used as consequences of the product formula, not as additional hypotheses.
Proof guide. Section 3 proves the trace identity, determinant linearisation,
and Adams–Möbius inversion behind Main Theorem I. Main Theorem II is
Theorem 3.33, proved from the same primitive channels under η < λ. Main
Theorem III is Theorem 3.42, proved by separating the additive Mertens input, the convergent product tail, the fixed-label Fourier coordinates, and the
branch-compatible determinant readout. The eta-gap and lower-order-tail
results are then used only as finite-cocycle boundary and stability diagnostics for these formulas.

the Peter–Weyl decomposition splits the Perron-point class profile into its
one-dimensional sector and its higher-dimensional sector. The span of all
cyclic quotient observables is exactly the one-dimensional sector; therefore
its projection is the abelian shadow, orthogonal to the genuine non-abelian
defect. Under η < λ, the full primitive class profile is generated by cyclic
quotient shadows exactly when every higher-dimensional primitive channel
Πϱ (λ−1 ) vanishes. These assertions are proved in Theorems 5.4, 5.8 and 5.10
and corollary 5.5.
Similarly, scalar records formed only from the fixed matrix A cannot by
themselves recover the non-trivial finite-group profile for a family of cocycles
over the same base. The projection boundary in Theorem 5.2 says that such
scalar data determine a single normalised class profile exactly when the nontrivial Perron-point channels Πτϱ (λ−1 ), ϱ ̸= 1, are already independent of
τ . Thus the scalar finite-part and cyclic-lift statements below serve only as
fixed-matrix normalisations and comparison tools; Corollary 5.3 gives the
corresponding two-cocycle separation.
An explicit S3 -extension separates the two obstruction types at the
level relevant to Main Theorem III. Its fixed-label Euler-transform bounds
Fε (1/2) ∈ (−381/1000, −380/1000) and Fρ (1/2) ∈ (−923/1000, −922/1000)
force the product-constant obstruction to naive Artin substitution. The
accompanying primitive bounds show that the sign-character trace vanishes
but its primitive value is non-zero by the quadratic Adams mechanism,
while the standard two-dimensional channel gives the rationally verified
higher-dimensional shadow defect. This gives a finite-state obstruction
model at the level of the determinant constants.
The quotient and shadow consequences are proved in Theorems 5.2, 5.4,
5.8, 5.10 and 5.14 and corollary 5.5; the canonical cyclic-lift root layer supplies only the scalar comparison boundary and is not an invariant of the
original finite-group cocycle.

Under the twisted spectral gap, the body proves the quotient-shadow description of the normalised primitive class profile in Theorems 5.4 and 5.8,
corollary 5.5, and lemmas 5.7 and 5.9. It includes scalar projection onto the
one-dimensional Peter–Weyl sector, functoriality for finite quotients, the orthogonal decomposition into abelian shadow and genuine non-abelian defect,
and exact detection of the one-dimensional sector by cyclic quotients of the
fixed cocycle. For every finite quotient q : G ↠ Q and every class function

The scalar root-of-unity determinant layers log det(I − ωqj zA)−1 occur in
the canonical cyclic-lift comparison model of Proposition 5.6 and lemma 5.7,
separate from the quotient pullbacks of the fixed cocycle.
The second scalar component is the fixed-matrix lift model, related to
inverse spectral questions for SFTs [21]. In this article it is used only as
finite-dimensional normalisation and as a comparison boundary separating
scalar cyclic-lift records from non-abelian class-profile invariants. When the
terminology of cyclic, torsion, or exponential scalar records appears below,
it serves only this boundary role; reconstruction or identifiability questions
for those records are not inputs to the theorem chain below.
1.4. Relation to existing work. The natural comparison class is the
symbolic-dynamics and dynamical ζ-function literature on prime cycles, finite extensions, and twisted determinant factorizations. In that literature
the leading Chebotarev distribution and Frobenius-class product asymptotics are classical inputs. Parry–Pollicott’s Chebotarev theorem for Axiom A Galois coverings [31], Noorani–Parry’s finite homogeneous-extension
theorem for shifts [27], Sharp’s Mertens theorem for Axiom A flows [41], and
Mesliza–Noorani’s Frobenius-class Mertens product theorem [25] supply the
classical comparison layer for Main Theorems II–III. The exponent |C|/|G|
and the existence of Frobenius-class products come from this lineage. The

FINITE-GROUP SFT EXTENSIONS AND PRODUCT CONSTANTS

11

theorem-local contribution is the finite Adams–Möbius determinant recovery
of the class constants after that classical leading term has been separated.
At the determinant level, the paper is closer to Fried’s twisted periodic
point formulae [13, 12], the twisted Perron–Frobenius framework of Adachi–
Sunada [1], and graph-Artin determinant theories of Hashimoto, Sunada,
Stark–Terras, and related finite-graph covering work [14, 15, 16, 47, 44,
45, 46]. Those works encode periodic or prime-cycle data by determinants
or representation-theoretic L-functions. The present finite-state calculation
fixes one finite-group SFT cocycle and asks the inverse question needed for
the constants: from the full family of twisted determinants, recover the primitive Peter–Weyl channels. The extra operation is the finite-group Adams
correction ψ m ϕ(g) = ϕ(g m ), forced by repetitions of a primitive orbit.
Finite group actions and extensions of SFTs form the adjacent symbolic
setting. Adler–Kitchens–Marcus [2], Fiebig [11], Silver–Williams [43], Boyle–
Schmieding [7], Boyle–Carlsen–Eilers [6], and Matsumoto [23] treat finite
actions, quotients, periodic data, and classification or obstruction questions.
Here the extension is fixed; the issue is not existence or flow-equivalence
classification but determinant-level recovery of Πϱ (λ−1 ), MC (A, τ ; G), and
the Adams-corrected Euler-product constants.
Recent finite-data and obstruction papers give a useful comparison for
what is finite and what is invariant in related dynamical settings. Coles’s
finite data criteria for shifts, Humbert’s first-Ruelle-resonance theorem for
Anosov flows with smooth potentials, O’Hare’s finite-data obstructions, and
the finite-state Artin/representation-theoretic examples of Crismale–Del
Vecchio–Griseta–Rossi [8, 17, 28, 9] provide comparison points for related
finite-data and obstruction problems. The present paper works with finite
twisted matrices, character-table orthogonality, and the stated Perron-point
gap η < λ, rather than with Banach-space resonance or reconstruction
machinery outside the fixed finite-matrix setting.
The comparison with the preceding literature is as follows. Periodic
twisted determinants and the Chebotarev/Mertens existence-and-exponent
layer are classical. The determinant-level contribution here is the finite
Adams–Möbius inverse formula that recovers primitive Peter–Weyl channels
rather than periodic Artin labels, and then evaluates the resulting additive
constants MC (A, τ ; G) under η < λ. The narrow priority claim for the
product theorem is the constant layer: Main Theorem III uses the classical
Frobenius-class product exponent and existence statement, but identifies the
remaining constant by the Adams-corrected fixed-label determinant formula.
The relevant object is the fixed-label Euler transform
X Πϱ (z r )
,
Fϱ (z) =
r
r≥1
its branch-compatible determinant expression, its Fourier readout into KC ,
and the obstruction to replacing it by the periodic Artin logarithm Lϱ . The
determinant/Adams/fixed-label formula is therefore the new constant-level
layer over the classical asymptotic form.

12

HAOBO MA

Thus this paper fixes one finite SFT cocycle and proves the determinant recovery of primitive Peter–Weyl channels, the Perron-point class Mertens constants, and the Adams-corrected fixed-label Euler-product constants. The
quotient, shadow, eta-gap, and S3 statements later in the paper are consequences and finite examples: they explain functoriality, scalar no-descent,
the boundary of the strict twisted gap, and a concrete obstruction to replacing Fϱ by Lϱ .
1.5. Plan of the paper. Section 2 fixes the symbolic, determinant, and
finite-group notation. Section 3 proves the Adams–Möbius primitive inversion: twisted determinants give primitive Peter–Weyl channels, Perron-point
evaluation gives Frobenius-class Mertens constants, and the fixed-label Euler
transform gives the Adams-corrected product constants. Section 4 studies
the strict twisted gap η < λ, the finite-dimensional hypothesis that permits
non-trivial channels to be evaluated at the Perron point. Section 5 develops
quotient functoriality, abelian shadows, cyclic detection, and the non-abelian
defect.
Section 6 isolates the scalar finite-part normalisation used for the trivial channel and proves the projection boundary for scalar comparison data.
The appendices give the S3 model computations Appendix A, the rational
interval estimates Appendix B, and the fixed-label rational enclosure data
Appendix C. These appendices supply the exact arithmetic behind the displayed S3 inequalities used in the Artin-substitution obstruction.
2. Preliminaries
The notation in this section follows the hierarchy of the paper. We first
fix primitive orbit counts for the base shift, then the finite-group extension
and Adams operations used in the main theorem chain. The reduced determinant, finite part, and fixed-matrix cyclic-lift notation come afterwards
because they serve only as scalar normalisation and a canonical comparison interface. Scalar variation, truncation certificates, secondary spectral
refinements, and transfer-operator extensions require additional hypotheses
beyond the theorem chain below.
Finite-data guide. All main objects are finite. The base datum is a
primitive adjacency matrix A of a finite edge shift, with Perron root λ =
ρ(A) > 0. A cocycle τ : EA → G assigns labels in a finite group. For
ϱ ∈ Irr(G), the twisted matrix Aϱ is the finite block transition matrix whose
traces record periodic labels in the ϱ-channel, and
η := max ρ(Aϱ )
ϱ̸=1

is the non-trivial twisted spectral radius, with the empty maximum read as
0. The strict inequality η < λ is an additional finite-matrix hypothesis in
Main Theorems II and III; it is not inferred from Main Theorem I. For a
class function ϕ, Πϕ (z) denotes the primitive fixed-label series, Lϕ (z) the
periodic Artin logarithm, and
X Πϕ (z r )
Fϕ (z) =
r
r≥1

FINITE-GROUP SFT EXTENSIONS AND PRODUCT CONSTANTS

13

the fixed-label Euler transform. The distinction between Fϕ and Lϕ is the
Adams distinction: repetitions change labels by g 7→ g r . The Adams operation is ψ r ϕ(g) = ϕ(g r ), and the Adams–Möbius coefficients are the finite
character-table coefficients obtained when each ψ r χπ is expanded in the
irreducible characters.
The Fourier convention is Hermitian. The forward class coefficient is
∗
χϱ (C) := χϱ (C) = χϱ (C −1 ), while inverse recovery uses χσ (C) without
conjugation. The scalar normalisations are C(A) = det′ (I − A/λ)−1 , FP(A),
and M(A) = γ + FP(A). The trivial determinant det(I − zA) is singular
at z = λ−1 , so the trivial channel is never evaluated there as an ordinary
determinant branch; it enters only through the finite part M(A) and the
reduced constant C(A). Non-trivial channels may be evaluated at z = λ−1
only after the strict gap η < λ has made their determinant branches regular.

The finite-group Adams–Möbius arguments only need the following uniform Perron–Frobenius trace bound. This is classical Perron–Frobenius
background for primitive non-negative matrices, stated here in the precise
finite-dimensional form used below: the Perron eigenvalue is simple, all other
eigenvalues lie strictly inside the Perron circle, and the trace is the sum of
the eigenvalue powers counted with algebraic multiplicity. Compare the
standard finite-matrix Perron–Frobenius reference [39] and the treatment of
mixing shifts of finite type in [21, 32]. The bound remains valid even when
λ = 1, and it is used only as a supporting finite-matrix estimate.

2.5. Standing notation. Throughout the paper ωq = e2πi/q denotes a
primitive qth root of unity, and Φq the qth cyclotomic polynomial. We
reserve the notation ϱ for irreducible representations of finite groups and
keep ρ(·) for spectral radii. Sections 3 and 5 use the graph and cocycle
picture for finite-group extensions. The later scalar section, Section 6, uses
only the primitive matrix formalism and cyclic lifts.
3. Adams–Möbius primitive inversion for finite-group
extensions
This section proves the finite-group primitive inversion results. Twisted
matrices Aϱ first linearise periodic data in Peter–Weyl coordinates; the
Adams–Möbius identity then removes repetitions of primitive orbits, where
labels change from g to g m . We use standard character-ring and Adamsoperation formalism as in [4, 40, 19], together with the classical Witt-vector
and necklace formalism for passing from repeated to primitive data [10, 24,
48].
The proof separates open-disc primitive inversion from the later Perronpoint evaluation. The first part, Lemma 3.6, theorem 3.7, and corollaries 3.10, 3.18 and 3.21, is the Adams–Möbius determinant and coefficient
mechanism and does not use a twisted spectral gap. The second block,
Theorems 3.33 and 3.79 and proposition 3.77, belongs to Main Theorem II
and starts only after the twisted spectral gap η = maxϱ̸=1 ρ(Aϱ ) < λ is imposed and converts the primitive channels into the class-indexed constants
MC (A, τ ; G), from which the channels are read back by non-abelian character orthogonality. The intervening class-decomposition formulas translate
between primitive Peter–Weyl coordinates and class counts.
Throughout the section, the underlying finite-state data are the data fixed
in Proposition 2.4; the additional boundary assumptions λ > 1 and η < λ
are invoked only in the statements where Perron-point values or Mertens
constants are evaluated.
3.1. Main Theorem I: Adams–Möbius primitive determinant linearisation. The first main argument has five steps. The twisted trace
identity converts matrices into periodic Peter–Weyl sums. The determinant linearisation rewrites those sums as logarithms of twisted determinants. The Adams–Möbius inversion then removes repetitions of primitive
orbits, and the irreducible-channel formula rewrites the result entirely in
terms of the determinant data. Finally, coefficient extraction gives the
primitive Peter–Weyl trace formula. The preliminary class-indicator and
block-diagonalisation propositions below set up this mechanism; they are
not separate main results.
For reference, the finite-dimensional passage is
twisted traces → determinant linearisation
→ Adams–Möbius inversion → irreducible channels
→ class-count recovery.
The next proposition records the finite construction with its hypotheses and
outputs, so that the later Perron-point arguments can cite the primitive
recovery in a single form.

FINITE-GROUP SFT EXTENSIONS AND PRODUCT CONSTANTS

21

Proposition 3.1 (Finite determinant recovery of primitive class counts).
The following implications prove the primitive determinant recovery without
any use of the Perron-boundary hypothesis η < λ.
(a) Lemma 3.6 and theorem 3.7 identify, for every class function ϕ, the periodic logarithm Lϕ (z) on |z| < λ−1 as a finite character-table linear combination of the determinant logarithms log det(I − zAπ )−1 , π ∈ Irr(G).
(b) Corollary 3.10 applies the Adams power maps in the character ring to
recover the primitive class series Πϕ (z) from those periodic determinant
logarithms.
(c) Corollary 3.18 and theorem 3.20 specialize the preceding identity to irreducible characters and give, respectively, the determinant-germ formula
and the coefficient formula for every primitive Peter–Weyl channel.
(d) Theorem 3.23 applies finite character orthogonality to those channels
and gives the primitive Frobenius-class counts from the same finite determinant and Adams data.
Consequently Main Theorem I is exactly the finite-state determinant construction consisting of the twisted matrices Aπ , the finite character table of
G, and the power maps on conjugacy classes.
Proof. Part (a) is the content of Lemma 3.6 and theorem 3.7: the trace identity expresses periodic labelled traces
in Peter–Weyl coordinates, and the
P
usual identity log det(I − zAπ )−1 = n≥1 Tr(Anπ )z n /n converts those traces
into determinant logarithms in the open Perron disc. Part (b) is exactly
Corollary 3.10; its proof is Möbius inversion of the primitive expansion with
the label changed by g 7→ g m , hence by the Adams operation ψ m . Part (c)
follows by substituting ϕ = χϱ and then either leaving the identity as a
determinant germ or extracting coefficients. The Adams coefficients are the
(m)
finite inner products aϱ,π of (31), so no analytic input beyond convergence
in |z| < λ−1 is introduced. Part (d) is the orthogonality inversion for the
basis of irreducible characters on class functions, applied coefficient by coefficient. These four steps are precisely the determinant, primitive-channel,
and class-recovery assertions claimed as Main Theorem I.
□

The next lemma is a finite-matrix/Peter–Weyl translation of the twisted
gap into the skew-product peripheral spectrum. It is included as a background checkable criterion for the hypotheses used later, not as a new
dynamical extension criterion; compare the finite homogeneous extension
framework of [26, 27].
Lemma 3.4 (Twisted gap as a skew-product peripheral-spectrum condition). Let Ae be the skew-product adjacency matrix attached to τ , and set
η := max ρ(Aϱ ).
ϱ̸=1

(i) η < λ.

has modulus strictly less than λ.

which is the displayed spectral decomposition. The trivial representation

gives
spec(A) ∩ {z ∈ C : |z| = λ} = {λ},
and the eigenvalue λ is simple.
Therefore η < λ holds exactly when every non-trivial block Aϱ has spectral
radius strictly less than λ. Since every eigenvalue of Aϱ has modulus at most
ρ(Aϱ ), this is equivalent to saying that every eigenvalue of every non-trivial
block has modulus strictly less than λ, which is statement (ii). Because
the trivial block contributes exactly one peripheral eigenvalue, namely the
simple eigenvalue λ, statement (ii) is equivalent to saying that the peripheral
spectral multiset of Ae consists of that single copy of λ. This is statement
e ∩ {z ∈ C : |z| = λ} = {λ} would not
(iii). A mere point-set identity spec(A)
exclude extra copies of λ coming from non-trivial blocks, so multiplicity is
essential. Finally, statement (iii) says precisely that Ae has spectral radius λ,
that λ is algebraically simple, and that every other eigenvalue has modulus
strictly less than λ, which is statement (iv).
Finally, if Ae is irreducible, then Perron–Frobenius theory for irreducible
non-negative matrices says that irreducibility together with the absence of
any peripheral eigenvalue other than the simple Perron eigenvalue is equivalent to primitivity. Thus, under irreducibility, statements (i)–(iv) are also
e
equivalent to primitivity of A.

Up to this point, the material is representation-theoretic infrastructure. The
specific content of the present paper begins when the determinant data are
pushed to the primitive level by Adams–Möbius inversion.
The next lemma fixes the trace convention used throughout the rest of
the section. It is the only place where the twisted matrices are identified
with fixed-point sums; afterwards all primitive formulas are obtained by
class-function expansion and Adams–Möbius inversion. Together with the
determinant linearisation and its primitive corollaries below, it forms Main
Theorem I from the introduction. The point of spelling it out separately
is that the passage from determinants to primitive data has two genuinely
distinct stages: matrix traces first give periodic Peter–Weyl sums, and only
then does Adams–Möbius inversion remove repetitions of primitive orbits.
Thus the identity in Lemma 3.6 is not a primitive-orbit statement; it is the
periodic input on which the primitive inversion operates.

Remark 3.9 (Where ordinary Möbius inversion stops). If ϕ = 1, the factor
ϕ(τ (γ)r ) in (16) is independent of r, and Corollary 3.10 reduces to the
usual scalar Möbius inversion for primitive orbit counts. For a non-trivial
class function this independence fails: the r-fold repetition changes the label
from g to g r . The Adams operator ψ r is exactly the correction needed for
this change. Thus the primitive finite-group step below is not obtained by
applying the scalar formula representation by representation; it uses the
Adams action on the character ring before Möbius inversion can remove
repetitions. For the general “primitive from repetitions” viewpoint, compare
the necklace/Witt formalism in [24, 48].

3.2. Fixed-label Euler transform versus periodic Artin logarithm.
The next theorem is the notational hinge between Main Theorem I and
the class-product constants. The periodic determinant logarithm Lϕ sums
repeated primitive labels as ϕ(τ (γ)r ), while the fixed-label Euler transform
F
keeps the primitive label fixed and then forms the Euler-product tail
Pϕ
r
r≥1 Πϕ (z )/r. Thus Πϕ is the primitive Peter–Weyl channel, Lϕ is the
Artin/periodic logarithm, and Fϕ is the quantity entering the Frobeniusclass product constant. For non-trivial characters its Perron-point value
is always read with the Abel-path branch convention of the determinant
formula below: the scalar Perron determinant is deleted only in the singular

30

HAOBO MA

triple (r, m, π) = (1, 1, 1), whereas scalar terms with rm ≥ 2 remain regular
and are retained.

Remark 3.15 (Relation to necklace/Witt inversion and twisted-zeta precedents). The cancellation in Corollary 3.10 is the finite-group analogue of
the classical “primitive from repetitions” mechanism in the necklace and
Witt-vector literature [24, 48]. What is specific here is the finite-state cocycle input: repetitions replace a primitive label g by g m , so the relevant
correction is not scalar Möbius inversion alone but the Adams operation
ψ m ϕ(g) = ϕ(g m ) inside the character ring of one fixed finite-group extension.
Twisted periodic-point formulas and determinant identities also appear in
Fried’s work [13, 12] and in the dynamical-zeta treatment of Parry–Pollicott
[32]. Those works, together with classical dynamical Mertens and Chebotarev product theorems, account for the leading exponent and the existence
scale of the Frobenius-class asymptotic. The claim made here begins after
that classical layer: the finite-state Adams–Möbius primitive inversion identifies the fixed-label determinant constant which enters the product, rather
than the periodic Artin logarithm.

The determinant identity above is the generating-function part of Main Theorem I. The next theorem is the coefficient recovery from the same identity,
while the following corollary is only the data-interface version: the theorem
gives the formula, and the corollary records that the finite twisted trace, or
determinant, data is exactly the input needed to use it. Neither the twisted
spectral gap nor cyclic root-of-unity reconstruction enters this first main
theorem.
Remark 3.19 (Abelian toy model: the cyclic lift). Let G = Z/qZ, and let
χj (h) = ωqjh be its irreducible characters. The canonical cyclic-lift cocycle
is the special cocycle τq : EA → Z/qZ with τq (e) = 1 on every edge; its
skew-product adjacency matrix is A ⊗ Pq . For this cocycle, and not for a
general Z/qZ-valued edge labelling, one has Aχj = ωqj A, and therefore
Lχj (z) = log det(I − ωqj zA)−1 .
The Adams–Möbius inversion (17) then reduces to the ordinary Fourier decomposition on the q-th roots of unity. This is the concrete bridge between
the abelian formulas of Section 6 and the present finite-group formalism, but
only after the original cocycle has been replaced by this canonical cyclic-lift
cocycle. This model is also not an example of the twisted spectral gap used
later when q > 1: for every non-trivial character χj , ρ(Aχj ) = ρ(A) = λ,
so the non-trivial twisted radius is η = λ, not < λ. The cyclic-lift layer
is therefore a finite-matrix comparison case, not a source of Perron-point
non-trivial-channel constants.

coefficient-level primitive data are finite-state data. More precisely, the
(m)
finite character table of G, the Adams coefficients aϱ,π , and the twisted

Proof. The explicit coefficient formula is exactly Theorem 3.20. The coeffi(m)

are known from the Adams power maps on conjugacy classes. The traces
Tr(Akπ ) are determined by the logarithmic derivative, or equivalently by the

Πϱ (z). The determinant-series formulation is the same statement before

□
This is the coefficient-level form of Main Theorem I, not a preliminary estimate: it is the point at which the finite twisted determinant family becomes
primitive orbit data. The trace lemma supplies the periodic input, the
linearisation theorem rewrites it as twisted determinant data, the Adams–
Möbius corollary removes repetitions, and the determinant corollary and
trace theorem give the primitive Peter–Weyl series and coefficients.
3.4. Bridge to classes and Main Theorem II. The primitive Peter–
Weyl coefficients determine the conjugacy-class counts by Fourier inversion
on G. The next proposition is a bridge: it translates the primitive channels
of Main Theorem I into class counts, but it does not add a new independent
main theorem.

If G is trivial, this maximum is empty and η = 0. Thus the formal determinant identities Theorems 3.7, 3.20 and 3.23 and corollaries 3.10 and 3.18
are unconditional in the open Perron disc, while the estimates and constants
from Lemma 3.25 through Theorem 3.79 are conditional on λ > 1 and the
displayed gap. The only scalar input used below is the trivial-channel orbitMertens constant M(A); its finite-matrix formula is proved independently
in Section 6.
The class Mertens theorem below is the analytic part of Main Theorem II
from the introduction. The leading density term is included to identify the
constant term and normalisation; the new ingredient in this section is the
passage from the twisted determinant channels to the primitive Perron-point
class profile. The later Fourier formula is a recovery of the constants by
character orthogonality. The proof is assembled from the next five lemmas:
class decomposition, the non-trivial channel estimate, absolute convergence
at the Perron point, a geometric-harmonic tail bound, and the trivial Perronchannel contribution.

Milestone: Perron-point constants. The primitive determinant channels
from Main Theorem I now become constants at z = λ−1 . This block first
records the additive class-Mertens readout under η < λ, then separates the
product-tail reduction needed for Frobenius-class Euler products.
3.5. Class Mertens constants and Adams-corrected Euler products.
The class-Mertens arguments have two outputs. First, Perron-point primitive Peter–Weyl coordinates give additive Frobenius-class Mertens constants.
Second, the fixed-label Euler transform gives the normalised Frobeniusclass Euler-product constants. The rigidity, finite-enclosure, and Artinsubstitution results below record consequences of these two formulas.
This subsection proves the finite-group Mertens constants used in Main
Theorems II–III. The additive constants come from Perron-point primitive
coordinates; the Euler-product constants come from the corresponding fixedlabel transform and its determinant evaluation. The finite-dimensional passage is
additive class constants
↓ product-tail reduction
fixed-label Euler transform
↓ finite determinant evaluation
determinant readout
↓ consequences
Artin obstruction.
Here Theorem 3.33 supplies the additive constants, while Theorems 3.11
and 3.42 give the product formula. The determinant layer then evaluates the
fixed-label vector, compares the resulting coordinates, and isolates the Artinsubstitution obstruction without introducing a second family of product
constants.
The two coordinates used throughout the subsection are Πϱ (λ−1 ), for
the additive constants, and Fϱ (λ−1 ), for the product constants. The periodic Artin logarithm Lϱ appears only as the comparison object in the final
obstruction theorem. The scalar normalisation separates in the two finite
Fourier correspondences
{MC }C ⇐⇒ {Πϱ (λ−1 )}ϱ̸=1 ,

{KC }C with C(A) ⇐⇒ {Fϱ (λ−1 )}ϱ̸=1 .

The first correspondence is Main Theorem II together with the Hermitian
class transform; the second is Main Theorem III together with the scalar
finite-part identity M(A) = γ + FP(A) = γ + log C(A). The proposition
below records the resulting finite determinant determination before the individual proofs begin.

product constants). Assume the standing hypotheses for the class-Mertens

The first arrow is Main Theorem I together with Theorem 3.33; the classMertens identity gives the next arrow; the fixed-label Euler transform gives
the product coordinates; and the final equivalence is the scalar-normalised

Consequently the finite determinant data, together with the stated branch and
scalar-normalisation conventions, determines the whole output of Main Theorems II–III in this determinant-coordinate chart. Conversely, once C(A)
is fixed and the same branch-compatible product-coordinate chart is used,

in this fixed scalar-normalised determinant model.
The finite-coordinate determination is literal. Let (A′ , τ ′ , G) be another
finite-group extension satisfying the same hypotheses with the same Perron
value λ, the same scalar normalisation C(A′ ) = C(A), and the same group
G. Suppose that the branch-compatible determinant values which occur in
Proposition 3.64 agree for the two systems, namely
Log det(I − λ−k Aπ )−1 = Log det(I − λ−k A′π )−1
for every k = rm ≥ 2 and every π ∈ Irr(G), and also for the Abel-path
non-trivial boundary values with k = 1 and π ̸= 1. Then the two extensions
have identical values

for all non-trivial ϱ and all conjugacy classes C. Conversely, if C(A′ ) =
C(A), the same branch-compatible product-coordinate chart is used, and the
positive product vectors (KC )C agree, then all non-trivial fixed-label Perron
coordinates Fϱ (λ−1 ) agree in that chart. Hence auxiliary scalar comparisons,
lower-order tail refinements, or notational substitutes can affect the main
constants only by changing either the scalar normalisation or one of these
non-trivial determinant boundary coordinates.
Proof. Main Theorem I expresses the primitive Peter–Weyl coefficient
πn,ϱ (A, τ ) by Adams–Möbius inversion from the finite twisted determinant

evaluates the Perron-point primitive values Πϱ (λ−1 ) for ϱ ̸= 1 along the
specified Abel path and gives the class-Mertens constants by (40). This
proves the first two arrows.

The fixed-label Euler transform is a convergent Adams-weighted transform of the primitive channels at the Perron point; this is precisely Theorem 3.11. Substituting those values into Theorem 3.42 gives the product
constants. After the scalar base C(A) has been separated, the normalised
logarithmic vector

has Fourier expansion K(C) = ϱ̸=1 χ∗ϱ (C)Fϱ (λ−1 ). Orthogonality of the

vector and the non-trivial fixed-label coordinate vector determine one another once the scalar normalisation and branch-compatible determinantcoordinate chart are fixed.
The final assertion follows immediately from the same inverse Fourier formula in the fixed chart. If a branch-compatible substitute has all the same
class constants after the same scalar normalisation, its normalised logarithmic vector is the same and therefore all non-trivial Fourier coordinates agree.
If the coordinates agree in that chart, the displayed Fourier expansion gives
the same normalised logarithmic vector and hence the same class constants.
The stability, comparison, and obstruction results later in the subsection use
only these coordinates, so they factor through the stated chain.
It remains to prove the two-extension source-closure assertion. The
(m)
Adams coefficients aϱ,π , the Möbius factors, and the class characters
depend only on the common finite group G. Therefore the determinant
formula of Proposition 3.64 is the same absolutely convergent linear
combination of the displayed branch-compatible determinant values for the
two systems. Equality of those values gives Fϱ (λ−1 ) = Fϱ′ (λ−1 ) for every
ϱ ̸= 1. The same determinant-value hypothesis applies to the boundary
formula of Lemma 3.76, so Πϱ (λ−1 ) = Π′ϱ (λ−1 ) for every non-trivial ϱ. The
class-Mertens formula (40) then gives equality of the additive constants
MC , and the product formula (59), together with C(A′ ) = C(A), gives
equality of all KC .
Conversely, if the scalar normalisations, branch-compatible productcoordinate conventions, and positive product vectors agree, then the
normalised logarithmic vectors K agree. Applying the inverse Fourier
formula (91), or equivalently (113), gives equality of every non-trivial
fixed-label coordinate in that chart. Thus an auxiliary record which leaves
the scalar normalisation and these determinant boundary coordinates
fixed is invisible to the main constants, while changing a main constant
necessarily changes one of those two pieces of data. This proves the stated
finite-coordinate closure.

The statements in the right-hand column do not introduce a further product constant, Perron branch, determinant coordinate, or quotient invariant.
Each either applies previously proved formulas, records a branch or convention choice, or identifies the functorial loss incurred when the already recovered primitive class profile is tested only through abelian or cyclic quotients.
The first three rows are exactly the finite determinant dependence proved in
Proposition 3.31. The eta-gap statements supply the Perron-point branch
criterion, and Theorems 5.8 and 5.10 explain what quotient shadows forget
after the profile has been recovered. Thus every output used by Main Theorems II–III and by the quotient-shadow results factors through the scalar
normalisation C(A) and the non-trivial fixed-label determinant coordinates
(Fϱ (λ−1 ))ϱ̸=1 .
Body statements behind the three main theorems. Under the hypotheses
stated in the introduction, the three displayed introduction theorems are
exactly the following body statements.
(i) Main Theorem I is the finite determinant interface of Proposition 3.1:
twisted traces give the class-function determinant logarithms, Adams–
Möbius inversion gives the primitive channels, and finite character
orthogonality gives the primitive Frobenius-class counts.
(ii) Main Theorem II is the Perron-boundary continuation of those same
primitive channels under λ > 1 and η < λ, namely Theorems 3.33
and 3.79 and proposition 3.77. It produces the class constants
MC (A, τ ; G) and their Hermitian Fourier–Parseval readout.
(iii) Main Theorem III is the fixed-label Euler-product evaluation Theorem 3.42 together with the determinant readout Propositions 3.64
and 3.66. The rigidity, comparison, and S3 obstruction statements
are consequences of this readout.
Consequently the introduction theorem environments summarize the body
statements listed above. No further hypothesis, boundary value, branch
convention, scalar normalisation, or appendix input is added in the summary,
and every output in Main Theorems I–III factors through the finite-data
chain of Proposition 3.31.
For (i), Proposition 3.1 is proved before any Perron-boundary assumption is imposed. Its proof cites precisely Lemma 3.6, theorems 3.7, 3.20

46

HAOBO MA

and 3.23, and corollaries 3.10 and 3.18. These statements establish, in order, the fixed-point trace identity, the finite determinant linearisation of
Lϕ , the Adams–Möbius identity for Πϕ , the irreducible determinant-germ
formula, the primitive Peter–Weyl coefficient formula, and class-count recovery by finite character orthogonality. This is exactly the content of the
first introduction theorem.
For (ii), imposing λ > 1 and η < λ gives the non-trivial Perron-point values of the primitive channels by Theorem 3.33. The same theorem proves the
displayed class-Mertens asymptotic and the formula (40); Proposition 3.77
and theorem 3.79 then identify the normalised deviation profile and its Hermitian Fourier–Parseval transform. These are exactly the additional assertions made in the second introduction theorem, and their only new analytic
hypothesis is the strict finite-dimensional twisted gap already stated there.
For (iii), Theorem 3.42 proves the Frobenius-class Euler-product asymptotic with the fixed-label transform Fϱ (λ−1 ) and the product constant KC .
The determinant evaluation and finite enclosure criterion for those fixedlabel Perron values are Propositions 3.64 and 3.66; substituting them into
the class product gives the determinant readout in Theorem 3.47 and corollary 3.49. The comparison, stability, scalar-baseline readout, and obstruction clauses are downstream consequences recorded in Proposition 3.55,
corollaries 3.48 and 3.52, and theorem 3.72. The cited S3 statements supply
concrete finite witnesses to that last obstruction. Finally, Proposition 3.31
proves that these three tiers are connected by this finite determinant dependence, so the introduction restatements introduce no independent theorem
beyond the assembled body arguments.
□
Remark 3.32 (Product-constant refinements). Under the hypotheses of Theorem 3.42, fix the scalar normalisation αA = γ +log C(A) and the non-trivial
Perron-boundary vector
F(A, τ ; G) := Fϱ (λ−1 ) ϱ̸=1 .

Then the hyperplane stability of Corollary 3.48, the assembled finite determinant formula and truncation of Corollary 3.49, the fixed-coordinate
fibre corollary Theorem 3.51, and the Parseval obstruction budget of Corollary 3.74 are downstream refinements of the Main Theorem III coordinate
vector (αA , F(A, τ ; G)). More precisely, after the periodic Artin comparison
vector (Lϱ (λ−1 ))ϱ̸=1 is fixed for the obstruction statements, each listed refinement is functorially determined by these already recovered coordinates.
Conversely, equality of the scalar-normalised fixed-coordinate product vector, or equality of the positive product constants with the same scalar normalisation, forces equality of F(A, τ ; G). Thus none of the listed refinements
supplies an independent main contribution, an additional hypothesis for
the product asymptotic, or a new source of class-product constants. For

Milestone: fixed-label product constants. The additive constants determine
the classical product exponent and scalar normalisation, but the remaining
product constant is controlled by the fixed-label Euler transform Fϱ , not by
the periodic Artin logarithm. The following files prove this product formula
and its finite Fourier rigidity.
3.5.2. Product-tail reduction and fixed-label Euler transform. This block converts the additive constants of Main Theorem II into the positive Frobeniusclass Euler-product constants of Main Theorem III. The tail reduction separates the scalar factor C(A) from the non-trivial class coordinates, and
the fixed-label Euler transform is the non-scalar input that remains. The
determinant evaluation in the next block computes exactly this fixed-label
vector.

Lemma 3.37 (Exponentiating the class product logarithm). Let a ∈ R,
L ∈ R, and let RN = O(N −1 ) be a real sequence. If a positive sequence QN
satisfies
log QN = −a log N + L + RN ,
then

QN = eL N −a 1 + O(N −1 ) .
If the implied constant in RN = O(N −1 ) is uniform over a finite set of
indices, then the resulting multiplicative error is uniform over that set.
Proof. Choose B > 0 with |RN | ≤ B/N for all large N . Then
QN = eL N −a eRN .
For all large N , |RN | ≤ 1, and the elementary estimate |ex − 1| ≤ e|x| for
|x| ≤ 1 gives eRN = 1 + O(N −1 ). The same bound with the maximum of
the finitely many constants gives the uniform assertion.
□

The product theorem has two ingredients. The exponent |C|/|G|, Chebotarev equidistribution, and the existence scale of Frobenius-class Mertens
products are the standard symbolic-dynamical inputs of Parry–Pollicott,
Noorani–Parry, Sharp, and Mesliza–Noorani [31, 27, 41, 25]. In the present
finite SFT model, closed orbits are primitive periodic edge-shift orbits, the
Frobenius datum is the conjugacy class of the ordered edge-cocycle product, and the product weight is λ−|γ| = exp(−h|γ|), with h = log λ. A
change from right to left homogeneous-extension conventions only replaces
C by C −1 ; the Hermitian coefficient χ∗ϱ (C) fixes the orientation used below.
The finite determinant contribution of this paper is the evaluation of the
non-trivial constant coordinates as the fixed-label Euler transform values
Fϱ (λ−1 ), not as the periodic Artin logarithms Lϱ (λ−1 ).
3.5.3. Main Theorem III: fixed-label Euler-product constants. The notation
in the theorem is the fixed-label notation of Section 3.2. Here KC is the
positive real product constant, C(A) is the scalar finite-part normalisation,
χ∗ϱ (C) = χϱ (C) is the Hermitian class coefficient, and the non-trivial coordinates are Fϱ (λ−1 ). The additive theorem supplies the r = 1 logarithm,
the primitive product-tail lemma supplies the r ≥ 2 repetitions, character
orthogonality gives the non-trivial coordinates, and the determinant formula
evaluates those coordinates.

The proof of Main Theorem III separates the following dependencies. The
Frobenius-class product existence statement and exponent |C|/|G| are the
classical symbolic-dynamical input recalled above. The scalar normalisation
is the finite-part constant C(A) from the trivial channel. The new finitestate part is the fixed-label calculation: the additive Mertens logarithm and
the absolutely convergent r ≥ 2 product tail are converted by class Fourier
orthogonality into the values Fϱ (λ−1 ), and Propositions 3.44 and 3.64 evaluate these values from the Adams–Möbius determinant formula. The rigidity
statements later in this section are consequences of this evaluated coordinate
vector, while the finite S3 interval computations in the appendices verify the
Artin-substitution obstruction in one model. The strict twisted gap η < λ
leaves only the scalar Perron pole; the non-trivial branches and the displayed value of KC are therefore fixed by the Adams-corrected determinant
calculation rather than by the periodic Artin logarithms.

Proposition 3.43 (Dependency map for Main Theorem III). Under the
hypotheses of Theorem 3.42, the determinant part of the product theorem
factors through the following ordered mathematical inputs:
(i) the classical Frobenius-class product layer of [41, 31, 27, 25] supplies
the existence of a product constant and the exponent |C|/|G|;
(ii) Lemma 3.35 converts the logarithmic class-Mertens estimate and the
absolutely convergent r ≥ 2 product tail into (58);
(iii) Lemmas 3.59 and 3.60 convert the primitive-tail constant into the
fixed-label character expression (59);
(iv) Theorem 3.11 and proposition 3.64 identify each non-trivial coordinate
Fϱ (λ−1 ) with its Adams–Möbius determinant boundary value, with the
scalar Perron triple deleted before evaluation;
(v) Propositions 3.44 and 3.55 and theorem 3.51 record the reversible
fixed-coordinate form and the separation between the inherited classical product theorem and the Adams-corrected determinant constant
proved here.
Consequently the Main Theorem III product constants are determined by the
scalar normalisation C(A), the finite character table and power maps of

60

HAOBO MA

G, and the finitely many twisted determinant polynomials det(I − zAπ ); replacing the fixed-label vector by any other branch-compatible vector changes
the class constants exactly by the Peter–Weyl error measured in Proposition 3.55.
Proof. Item (i) is precisely the inherited Frobenius-class product layer in
(57): it supplies the existence of KC and the density exponent |C|/|G| before any determinant evaluation is made. Item (ii) is Lemma 3.35, which
separates the class-Mertens logarithm from the absolutely convergent r ≥ 2
product tail and gives (58). Item (iii) is the finite character-table rewrite in
Lemmas 3.59 and 3.60; these lemmas introduce no new analytic limit, only
the fixed-label Fourier coordinates with the stated Hermitian normalisation.
Item (iv) is the determinant boundary evaluation proved in Theorem 3.11
and proposition 3.64. The scalar Perron logarithm is not assigned a finite
part there: the triple (1, 1, 1) has zero coefficient in every non-trivial fixedlabel coordinate and is deleted before the Abel endpoint is taken. Hence
each non-trivial value Fϱ (λ−1 ) is fixed by the branch-compatible twisted
determinant data. Item (v) is the reversible coordinate statement of Proposition 3.44, together with the obstruction skeleton and comparison map in
Theorem 3.51 and proposition 3.55. Substituting the coordinates obtained
in item (iv) into the product formula therefore determines all constants
KC , and the comparison proposition shows exactly how a different branchcompatible fixed-label vector would change them. This proves both the
successive recovery statement and the final determination statement.
□

The exponent |C|/|G| and the existence of a Frobenius-class product constant are the inherited classical part of Theorem 3.42, parallel to the dynamical Mertens and Chebotarev product theorems of Sharp, Parry–Pollicott,
Noorani–Parry, and especially Mesliza–Noorani’s Frobenius-class Mertens
product theorem [41, 31, 27, 25]. In applying those results here, the abstract
objects in the cited theorems are identified with the primitive finite-state
+
data fixed in Section 2: the mixing base is the primitive edge shift (XA
, σ),
the finite extension is the skew-product graph on V × G, the Frobenius
class is the intrinsic conjugacy class of τ (γ), and the entropy normalisation
is h = log λ. The possible opposite-action convention in a homogeneous
extension only replaces C by C −1 ; the exponent is unchanged and our Hermitian coefficient χ∗ϱ (C) fixes the displayed class orientation. The strict
inequality η < λ supplies the required absence of additional unit-modulus
or Perron-boundary twisted channels in this finite-dimensional model. No
broader smooth, Markov-extension, or arbitrary-cocycle hypothesis is used
in the sequel. The derivation above proves the displayed KC -formula from
Main Theorem II and the r ≥ 2 product tail; the new assertion used in the

the fixed-label Euler transform by an ordinary Artin logarithm.

Proof. The determinant identity (67), including the local deletion of (1, 1, 1),
the Abel-boundary convention for non-trivial rm = 1 terms, the ordinary
interior evaluation for rm ≥ 2, and the absolute convergence of the remaining tail, is exactly Proposition 3.64. Hence the numbers Dϱ are fixed by the
stated finite determinant data.
The leading product exponent and the existence of the positive constants
are exactly Theorem 3.42; the comparison with the existing product literature is stated in Proposition 3.55. The character formula (59) expresses
(KC )C in terms of C(A) and the non-trivial values Fϱ (λ−1 ). Replacing
those values by Dϱ using (67), and then taking logarithms in the positive
real branch fixed by Theorem 3.42, gives the first identity in (70). Averaging
this identity over conjugacy classes with weights |C|/|G| gives the second
identity in (69), because every non-trivial irreducible character is orthogonal to the trivial character. Multiplying the definition of HK by |C|/|G|
and summing over classes then gives the product constraint in (69). The inverse Fourier identity in (70) and the Parseval identity (72) are the standard
Hermitian character orthogonality relations for the zero-mean class-function
space.
Solving the definition (68) for KC and substituting the first identity in
(70) gives (71). The same calculation proves the classification map (73):
starting from a non-trivial Fourier vector in the real class-function form
gives a zero-mean class function by Fourier inversion, exponentiation gives
positive constants, and the weighted product is exp(−αA ) because the zeromean condition cancels the non-scalar summand in the logarithmic product.
Conversely, a vector in KαA gives the zero-mean logarithmic class function
appearing in (74), and Hermitian character orthogonality recovers exactly
its non-trivial Fourier coordinates. These two constructions are inverse to
each other, and Parseval gives the stated isometry; the only constraints
are therefore the weighted product condition and the conjugacy-pair reality
condition for a real class function.
This proves that the product vector is determined by the listed scalar
normalisation, character table, Adams tensors, branch tags, and twisted
determinant polynomials. Conversely, if the positive constants and C(A)

67

are known, then (68) constructs HK , and the inverse Hermitian Fourier
formula in (70) recovers each Dπ . The final equal-data assertion follows by
applying the same displayed formulae to the two systems. No step uses any
presentation, cover, fold, gauge, or cocycle-conjugacy invariant beyond these
fixed coordinates, which proves the asserted scope of the rigidity statement.

is equivalent, in the positive logarithm branch, to Eb (C) = 0 for every
class; by Parseval this is equivalent to bϱ = Dϱ for every non-trivial ϱ.
Finally, the weights |C|/|G| are positive and sum to one, so if every class
had |Eb (C)| < δ, the weighted square average in (75) would be strictly less
than δ 2 , contradicting the definition of δ. This proves

3.5.5. Local equivalence of product coordinates. The next consequences describe the product-coordinate vector recovered in Main Theorem III. They
identify the finite Fourier coordinates of the Adams-corrected positive class
constants inside the scalar-normalised determinant chart fixed above.
Proposition 3.50 (Fixed-coordinate equivalence after scalar normalisation). Under the hypotheses and notation of Theorem 3.47, consider two finite determinant-coordinate data sets with the same finite group G, the same
character table and Adams tensors, the same scalar normalisation C(A), and
the same normalized Abel/ordinary branch convention of Proposition 3.64.
Thus equality of a coordinate always means equality of the corresponding
branch-compatible logarithmic determinant value, not merely equality of an
exponentiated determinant. If their non-trivial fixed-label Perron vectors
agree,
Fϱ (λ−1 ) = Fϱ′ (λ−1 )
(ϱ ̸= 1),
then they give the same Frobenius-class product constants KC = KC′ for
every conjugacy class C. Conversely, if the constants agree for every C and
the same scalar normalisation C(A) is used, then the non-trivial fixed-label
Perron vectors agree. Thus the recovered invariant at Main Theorem III is
the fixed branch-normalised determinant-coordinate vector modulo this finite
Fourier equivalence.
Proof. With C(A) fixed, equality of the fixed-label Perron vectors makes
the right-hand side of (70) identical for every class. Substitution in the
exponential formula for KC in Theorem 3.47 therefore gives KC = KC′ for
all C. Conversely, equality of the positive constants and equality of C(A)

Hence a non-zero non-trivial boundary error cannot be hidden, inside these

partial fixed-coordinate record of rank less than q − 1 necessarily admits
the affine kernel family above; the full Adams-corrected Peter–Weyl determinant boundary is the coordinate record used to close this finite linearalgebra failure. This statement is a structural refinement subordinate to
Main Theorem III: it assumes the scalar-normalised product constants and
their Adams-corrected Peter–Weyl determinant boundary have already been
identified, and it adds no new product asymptotic, determinant evaluation,
general observation-system theory, claim, or Perron hypothesis to Main Theorem III.
Proof. First observe that dim HG = q − 1, because the real vector space of
class functions has dimension q and the displayed weighted-mean condition
is one non-zero real linear equation. The image of Kα lies in the stated
product hyperplane: multiplying (86) over all conjugacy classes gives
Y

HL (C) := −

|G|
log LC − α
|C|

has weighted mean zero, exactly because C LC = e−α , and Kα (HL ) =
L. Thus Kα parametrises the whole hyperplane. It is injective because
equality of the positive vectors Kα (H) = Kα (H ′ ), followed by the ordinary
real logarithm in each coordinate, gives (|C|/|G|)(H(C) − H ′ (C)) = 0 for
every class C, hence H = H ′ .
Now let R : HG → W have the stated determining property. If V ∈ ker R,
then R(V ) = R(0), so the determining property gives Kα (V ) = Kα (0). By
injectivity of Kα , V = 0. Thus R is injective, and its rank is at least
dim HG = q − 1, proving (87).
Conversely, if a fixed-coordinate linear record is not injective, choose a
non-zero V ∈ ker R. For every H ∈ HG and every t ∈ R, linearity gives
R(H + tV ) = R(H). If s ̸= t, then H + sV ̸= H + tV ; by the injectivity
of Kα , already proved above, Kα (H + sV ) ̸= Kα (H + tV ). Thus every nonzero kernel vector produces the advertised one-parameter ambiguity family
of indistinguishable fixed-coordinate records with distinct positive product
constants. Taking, for instance, H = 0, s = 0, and t = 1 gives the stated
bad pair. Conversely, if two points H, H ′ ∈ HG have the same record,
then V := H ′ − H lies in ker R and the pair belongs to the affine family
H + tV . This proves that, in the fixed linear chart under discussion, the
displayed kernel lines account for all failures of a linear record to distinguish
the scalar-normalised product vectors. It also shows that the rank lower
bound is precisely the finite-dimensional injectivity condition needed in this
determinant-coordinate chart.
The finite character orthogonality relations identify the non-trivial irreducible characters with an orthonormal basis of the zero-mean class-function

space, with the standard conjugation symmetry when the original class function is real. Therefore the Peter–Weyl record (88) is an isometric injection
from HG onto a real coordinate space of dimension q − 1, so it attains the
lower bound.
For the dynamical constants, Theorem 3.47 identifies the coordinate tuple
of HK with (Fϱ (λ−1 ))ϱ̸=1 , and Proposition 3.64 evaluates those coordinates
from the finite twisted determinant family. This gives the claimed fixedcoordinate support for the determinant data used in Main Theorem III.
Finally, EB is the zero-mean class function whose non-trivial Fourier coordinates are Fϱ (λ−1 ) − Bϱ . Applying Parseval in the same zero-mean Peter–
Weyl space proves (89). If the right-hand side is non-zero, some classwise error is non-zero, so the substitute boundary cannot give all scalar-normalised
product constants; scalar data live in the trivial one-dimensional summand
and have already been fixed by α.

3.5.6. Finite determinant determination of the constants. We now identify
the determinant-to-constant map: the Adams-corrected fixed-label coordinates are computable from the finite twisted determinant family with the
stated branch convention.
Proposition 3.53 (Finite determinant determination of the main constants). Assume λ > 1 and the twisted spectral gap η < λ of Theorem 3.33.
Encode the finite group by its multiplication table and let the finite
determinant datum be


D(A, τ ; G) := G, {C ⊂ G conjugacy class}, (C, m) 7→ C m ,


C(A), {det(I − zAπ ) : π ∈ Irr(G)} .
Together with the Abel-path branch convention fixed separately in Theorem 3.33 and proposition 3.64, this finite datum determines the following
three nested output tiers. Each tier is asserted only under the hypotheses
and branch conventions of the theorem result cited for that tier.
(i) Algebraic determinant recovery: every primitive Peter–Weyl coefficient πn,ϱ (A, τ ) and hence every primitive class count pn,C ;
(ii) Perron-boundary recovery under the twisted gap: every non-trivial
Perron-point primitive value Πϱ (λ−1 ) and every class-Mertens constant MC (A, τ ; G);
(iii) Fixed-label product recovery: every fixed-label value Fϱ (λ−1 ) and every Frobenius-class Euler-product constant KC .
The recovery in item (i) is purely algebraic: coefficient extraction from the
finitely many determinant polynomials, finite character-table linear algebra,
Adams coefficients, Möbius inversion, and finite Fourier inversion. The
recovery in items (ii) and (iii) is the branch-compatible Perron-boundary
evaluation of those same finite determinant polynomials under η < λ, not
a claim that the boundary constants are finite algebraic expressions in the
polynomial coefficients alone. The singular scalar determinant logarithm
is never evaluated; the scalar normalisation uses the positive Perron constant C(A). Conversely, after the scalar normalisation C(A), the productconstant vector (KC )C determines exactly the non-trivial fixed-label Perron
vector (Fϱ (λ−1 ))ϱ̸=1 by the inverse Fourier formula in (91). Hence two
extensions with identical data D(A, τ ; G), the same branch choices, and
the same scalar normalisation C(A) have identical outputs in Main Theorems I–III. More precisely, the raw determinant recovery is Corollary 3.18
and theorem 3.23, the Perron-point convergence and additive constants are
Theorems 3.33 and 3.79, and the product-level fixed-label constants are Theorems 3.42 and 3.47 and proposition 3.64.
Proof. For tier (i), the finite group component of D(A, τ ; G) gives the complete character table, the irreducible characters, conjugacy classes, and the
Adams class maps C 7→ C m . The determinant polynomials
determine all
P
traces Tr(Anπ ) by the finite identity log det(I − zAπ )−1 = n≥1 Tr(Anπ )z n /n
on its formal power-series side. Lemma 3.6 identifies these traces with the

FINITE-GROUP SFT EXTENSIONS AND PRODUCT CONSTANTS

75

periodic twisted fixed-point sums. Finite character orthogonality then gives
Theorem 3.7, and the Adams–Möbius formula in Corollary 3.10 converts
the periodic class-function coefficients to primitive coefficients. Therefore
Corollaries 3.18 and 3.21 and theorem 3.23 recover all primitive Peter–Weyl
coefficients and primitive class counts from D(A, τ ; G).
For tier (ii), under η < λ, the non-trivial determinant series are regular at the Perron point along the branch specified in Theorem 3.33. That
theorem, together with Proposition 3.77 and theorem 3.79, evaluates the
non-trivial primitive values Πϱ (λ−1 ) and hence the class-Mertens constants.
For tier (iii), the fixed-label Euler-transform theorem Theorem 3.42 and the
finite determinant formula Proposition 3.64 evaluate Fϱ (λ−1 ) for every nontrivial ϱ; substituting these values into Theorem 3.47 gives the Frobeniusclass product constants KC . The scalar singular determinant is not evaluated in these steps: the scalar contribution is the supplied Perron constant C(A), exactly as in the displayed product formulas of Theorems 3.42
and 3.47. Thus the proof uses exactly the branch conventions and convergence hypotheses stated in the corresponding theorem of each tier.
Finally, the inverse formula (91) is a finite Fourier inversion on the class
algebra after the scalar baseline has been fixed. It shows that the vector
(KC )C determines precisely the non-trivial vector (Fϱ (λ−1 ))ϱ̸=1 , and the direct product formula gives the converse dependence. Every operation used
above is therefore either finite coefficient extraction, finite character-table
algebra, Adams–Möbius inversion, or the already proved branch-compatible
Perron-point evaluation under η < λ. The proposition adds no new leading
asymptotic claim beyond the cited results: the Frobenius-density exponent
in the product theorem is classical, while the new finite-data content used
here is the Adams–Möbius fixed-label determinant recovery of the constants.
Identical finite collections with identical branch choices and scalar normalisation pass through the same sequence of operations, so they have identical
outputs in the three main theorems.
□
Proposition 3.54 (Successive finite recovery of the three families of constants). Under the hypotheses of Proposition 3.53, the finite-data recovery
factors through the following ordered sequence:
(90)

D(A, τ ; G) −→ OI −→ OII −→ OIII ,

where OI consists of the primitive Peter–Weyl coefficients and primitive class
counts, OII consists of the non-trivial Perron-point primitive values and
class-Mertens constants, and OIII consists of the fixed-label Perron values
and Frobenius-class product constants. The first arrow uses only coefficient
extraction, Adams coefficients, and finite Fourier inversion. The second
arrow is the Abel-branch Perron-boundary evaluation supplied by the twisted
spectral gap. The third arrow is the fixed-label Euler-transform and classproduct formula of Theorem 3.42 and Theorem 3.47. Thus Proposition 3.53
and corollary 3.49 give the finite recoveries of Main Theorems I–III, while
Theorem 3.47 is the branch-compatible fixed-coordinate rigidity theorem for
the Adams-corrected product vector after scalar normalisation. In particular,
equality of two finite determinant data implies equality of all three ordered

76

HAOBO MA

outputs, and after C(A) is fixed equality of the product-constant vectors is
equivalent to equality of the non-trivial fixed-label Perron vectors.
Proof. The first arrow is exactly the finite extraction step in Proposition 3.53:
from D(A, τ ; G) one reads the twisted determinant polynomials, computes
Adams coefficients from the finite character and power tables, and applies
finite Fourier inversion to obtain the primitive Peter–Weyl coefficients and
primitive class counts. These are the entries collected in OI .
The second arrow uses the same determinant polynomials with the Abel
branch fixed by the twisted spectral gap η < λ. The determinant boundary
evaluations in Theorem 3.33 and proposition 3.64 give the non-trivial Perronpoint primitive values, and the class-Mertens constants are then the finite
character-table combinations stated in the Main Theorem II formula. These
are precisely the data denoted OII .
The third arrow is Main Theorem III. The fixed-label Euler transform
and the determinant formula of Theorem 3.42 and proposition 3.64 evaluate the non-trivial fixed-label Perron values, with the scalar Perron triple
deleted before boundary evaluation. Substitution into Theorem 3.47 gives
the Frobenius-class product constants, which form OIII . Therefore identical
finite determinant data, with the same branch convention and scalar normalisation, pass through identical finite operations and yield identical outputs
in all three tiers.
Finally fix C(A), equivalently the scalar baseline γ + log C(A). The forward product formula expresses each KC as the exponential of that scalar
baseline plus the class Fourier transform of the non-trivial fixed-label vector. The inverse formula (91) is ordinary finite Fourier inversion on the
class algebra, so the product vector determines exactly the non-trivial fixedlabel vector and conversely. This proves the asserted equivalence after scalar
normalisation.
□
The factor N −|C|/|G| and the existence of a Frobenius-class Mertens product asymptotic are the classical part of (57); compare the Frobenius-class
product theorem of Mesliza–Noorani [25], Sharp’s dynamical Mertens theorem [41], and the Chebotarev orbit-distribution antecedents [31, 27]. Nearby
finite-data and skew-product works such as [9, 28] address different rigidity
or extension questions. The finite-state primitive-label contribution needed
here is the following named comparison theorem: it is the Adams correction
to the Frobenius-class Mertens product, not merely a restatement of the
classical density theorem.
Proposition 3.55 (Adams correction to Frobenius-class Mertens products).
Under the hypotheses of Theorem 3.42, the following assertions hold after the
classical asymptotic form and the scalar base constant have been separated.
For the topologically mixing SFT defined by A, with finite group extension
determined by τ , primitive closed orbits weighted by λ−|γ| , and Frobenius
class restricted to a conjugacy class C ⊂ G, the product existence and the
leading exponent |C|/|G| are exactly the classical Frobenius-class product

FINITE-GROUP SFT EXTENSIONS AND PRODUCT CONSTANTS

77

layer supplied by Mesliza–Noorani’s theorem on Mertens products in Frobenius classes [25], in the Parry–Pollicott/Noorani–Parry Chebotarev symbolicextension setting and with Sharp’s symbolic-dynamical Mertens normalisation [31, 27, 41]. The comparison is made in the primitive closed-orbit convention fixed in Proposition 2.1: a length-n primitive orbit contributes the
factor (1 − λ−n ), and the Frobenius class is the conjugacy class of the finite return product of the edge cocycle. Thus the entropy weight e−hn in
the flow/SFT literature is the present λ−n , and the density exponent is the
class density |C|/|G|. If a cited homogeneous-extension convention uses the
opposite action, the return element is inverted and the comparison uses C −1
instead of C; this changes neither |C|/|G| nor the existence of the classical product layer, and our formula fixes the chosen orientation by using
χ∗ϱ (C) throughout. Throughout this paper that cited layer is treated as background: in this notation it is the assertion that PC (N )N |C|/|G| has a positive
finite limit, after the same Perron normalisation by λ; the sharper finitedimensional gap hypotheses of Theorem 3.33 give the explicit 1 + O(N −1 )
remainder used here. This proposition does not assert the Frobenius-class
Mertens asymptotic itself as new. The finite-state assertion of this proposition is the remaining fixed-coordinate statement: once that classical layer
and the scalar base γ+log C(A) are fixed, the determinant constant is exactly
controlled by the fixed-label values Fϱ (λ−1 ), these values are recovered from
finite twisted determinants by Adams–Möbius inversion, and every proposed
replacement is measured by the zero-mean Peter–Weyl error vector below.
Thus the new datum is the Adams-corrected fixed-label determinant constant
and its obstruction to Artin substitution, not the Frobenius-class Mertens asymptotic itself. The substitution test is downstream and is not a hypothesis
for Main Theorems I–III.
