Corollary 3.57 (Classical-layer uniqueness of the Adams coordinate). Assume the hypotheses of Theorem 3.42, and fix the classical Frobenius-class
product layer for every conjugacy class: the primitive-orbit product exists
with exponent |C|/|G|, and the scalar Perron normalisation is the fixed number γ + log C(A). Let B = (Bϱ )ϱ̸=1 be any branch-compatible non-trivial
Peter–Weyl boundary vector, and let KC (B) be the corresponding constants
from Proposition 3.55. If
KC (B) = KC

for every conjugacy class C ⊂ G,

while the exponent and scalar normalisation are kept fixed, then
Bϱ = Fϱ (λ−1 )

(ϱ ̸= 1).

Equivalently, once the classical existence and exponent layer has been removed, the only branch-compatible determinant coordinate vector that produces the true class-product constants is the Adams–Möbius fixed-label vector.

82

HAOBO MA

In particular the Artin-periodic replacement gives the same constants for all
classes if and only if Lϱ (λ−1 ) = Fϱ (λ−1 ) for every non-trivial irreducible
representation.
Proof. Apply the Parseval identity (93) in Proposition 3.55. The assumed
equality KC (B) = KC , with the same positive logarithm branch and the
same scalar normalisation, gives EB (C) = 0 for every conjugacy class. Hence
the left-hand side of (93) is zero, and so every summand |Fϱ (λ−1 ) − Bϱ |2
on the right-hand side is zero. Therefore Bϱ = Fϱ (λ−1 ) for all non-trivial
ϱ. Taking Bϱ = Lϱ (λ−1 ) gives the final assertion, which is also the equality
criterion in (92).
□
Thus Main Theorems I–III are finite determinant-to-primitive, Perronpoint, and Adams-corrected product-constant theorems. The classical
Chebotarev–Mertens comparison layer supplies the Frobenius-class product
existence, leading density, and exponent |C|/|G|. The new assertion
here is the determinant readout of the remaining constant: the scalar
Perron channel is separated, the singular triple (1, 1, 1) is deleted before
boundary evaluation, and the non-trivial branch-compatible coordinates
are the Adams-corrected fixed-label values Fϱ (λ−1 ). The shadow results
Theorems 5.4, 5.8 and 5.10, the eta-gap tests Theorem 4.1, corollary 4.2,
and proposition 4.6, and the S3 rational checks Theorem 5.14, propositions 5.15 and A.4, and corollary 5.16 serve this determinant and
product-coordinate picture. They are not asserted as new geometric
periodic-orbit invariants, Ruelle-resonance theorems, finite-data rigidity
theorems, or operator-algebraic classifications.
Proposition 3.58 (Comparison with existing symbolic-dynamical results).
The main results separate the classical symbolic-dynamical inputs from the
finite-label determinant assertions as follows. In each item below the cited
external layer supplies the indicated existence, distribution, leading exponent,
normalisation, or comparison context. The contribution of the present paper
is not a new Chebotarev exponent or a new existence theorem for Frobeniusclass products; it is the finite-state determinant formula for the residual
constant, with the Adams correction and branch convention made explicit.
(i) Main Theorem I and Corollaries 3.18 and 3.21 and theorem 3.23
use Parry–Pollicott [31] and Noorani–Parry [27] for the classical finite symbolic-extension setting; the asserted contribution is the finite
Peter–Weyl determinant inversion with Adams–Möbius correction for
primitive repetitions.
(ii) Main Theorem II and Theorems 3.33 and 3.79 and proposition 3.77
use Sharp’s Mertens scale [41] and the Parry–Pollicott/Noorani–Parry
Chebotarev lineage for the leading density, exponent, and strict-gap
context; the asserted contribution is the class-indexed constant obtained from non-trivial primitive-channel Perron values.
(iii) Main Theorem III and Theorems 3.42 and 3.72 and proposition 3.64 use the Frobenius-class product comparison, in particular
the Mesliza–Noorani Frobenius-class Mertens product theorem [25],
for product-existence, the Frobenius-class exponent |C|/|G|, and
normalisation context; the asserted contribution is the fixed-label

FINITE-GROUP SFT EXTENSIONS AND PRODUCT CONSTANTS

83

Euler transform Fϱ (z) = r≥1 Πϱ (z r )/r, its determinant evaluation
at z = λ−1 , and the obstruction to substituting the ordinary periodic
Artin logarithm Lϱ (λ−1 ) for Fϱ (λ−1 ).
(iv) The eta-gap, shadow, and S3 rational-check results complement the
same determinant/product-coordinate picture.
Coles’s geometric
periodic-orbit invariants [8], Humbert’s first-resonance theorem [17],
O’Hare’s finite-data rigidity result [28], and the non-commutative
skew-product framework of Crismale–Del Vecchio–Griseta–Rossi [9]
concern neighbouring problems and provide comparison points.
Consequently the main results consist of the finite Peter–Weyl inversion,
Perron-boundary fixed-label constants, Adams-corrected determinant formula
for the residual product constant, and Artin-substitution obstruction, with
the external results providing the surrounding Chebotarev–Mertens existence,
exponent, and modern comparison context.
P

Proof. The assertion follows by reading the cited theorem statements in
the order of the main results. Main Theorem I and the Peter–Weyl trace
and determinant corollaries are the only places where primitive finite-label
inversion is asserted; the external Chebotarev references cited in item (i)
supply the surrounding symbolic-extension and equidistribution setting,
not the Adams–Möbius determinant inversion displayed there. Main
Theorem II and the class Mertens formula of item (ii) use the classical
Mertens scale and strict-gap context only to place the leading term and
justify Perron-boundary evaluation; the class-indexed residual term is then
the finite Fourier expression proved in the displayed determinant formulas.
Main Theorem III and the product interfaces in item (iii) separate product
existence and exponent normalisation from the new fixed-label Euler
transform, whose Adams-corrected coordinates are exactly those proved
in Theorems 3.42 and 3.72 and proposition 3.64. Finally, the eta-gap,
quotient-shadow, and S3 results cited in item (iv) are invoked elsewhere
in the paper only through the determinant/product-coordinate interfaces
named in that item. These four comparisons account for the external inputs
used by the main results and prove the stated comparison.

Milestone: determinant readout and obstruction. The last part converts the
fixed-label product vector back to finite twisted determinants and records
the precise boundary where naive Artin substitution fails. This is the obstruction layer used by the S3 witness, not a new family of product constants.

3.5.8. Determinant evaluation of fixed-label Perron values. This is the
determinant-evaluation step in Main Theorem III. The preceding product
arguments reduce the product constants to the fixed-label numbers Fϱ (λ−1 );
this block evaluates those numbers from the same finite twisted determinant
data used in Main Theorem I. The branch convention is the one announced
in the subsection overview and made precise in the first lemma below:
Abel continuation for the regular non-trivial boundary terms, ordinary
interior-disc logarithms for rm ≥ 2, and deletion of the unique singular
scalar Perron triple before any endpoint logarithm is evaluated. This
local deletion convention is the one later cited in the statement of Main
Theorem III and in the fixed-label certificate appendix.
Throughout this subsubsection the following convention is in force. The
symbol Log det(I − zAπ )−1 denotes the normalized logarithmic germ equal
to 0 at z = 0, continued only along the Abel segment that is explicitly
present in the formula. For rm = 1 and π ̸= 1, the strict gap ρ(Aπ ) < λ
makes this continuation regular at z = λ−1 . For rm ≥ 2, the point z = λ−rm
lies strictly inside the scalar Perron disc and the ordinary convergent powerseries logarithm is used. The scalar Perron value Log det(I − λ−1 A1 )−1
is never assigned a finite part in a non-trivial fixed-label coordinate; the
corresponding triple (1, 1, 1) has coefficient zero by character orthogonality
and is deleted before the boundary limit is taken.

88

HAOBO MA

The determinant form of Main Theorem III uses one fixed normalization.
The sign of KC is the outer minus sign in (59), equivalently the positive
exponential of the negative logarithmic constant in (94). Class indicators
use the coefficient (|C|/|G|)χ∗ϱ (C), with inverse Fourier recovery performed
by summing against χσ (C). In Fϱ (λ−1 ), the single scalar boundary triple
(r, m, π) = (1, 1, 1) is omitted before evaluation because its coefficient is
zero for ϱ ̸= 1. Non-trivial rm = 1 determinants use the Abel boundary
value of the normalized germ at the origin; all rm ≥ 2 determinants use the
ordinary interior-disc value. These conventions are exactly those proved in
Lemmas 3.60, 3.62 and 3.63 and proposition 3.64, and the absolute majorant
in Lemma 3.63 justifies the displayed order of summation and Abel limiting.
Corollary 3.65 (Convention compatibility for Main Theorem III). In Main
Theorem III, the following product-constant conventions are forced and mutually compatible.
(i) The class-indicator coefficient of a non-trivial irreducible character is
(|C|/|G|)χ∗ϱ (C), where χ∗ϱ (C) = χϱ (C).
(ii) The product constant uses the single outer minus sign in (59), with
scalar base γ + log C(A).
(iii) The determinant formula for Fϱ (λ−1 ), ϱ ̸= 1, deletes only the scalar
Perron triple (r, m, π) = (1, 1, 1) before evaluation; non-trivial boundary terms use the Abel branch from the germ at the origin and all
rm ≥ 2 terms use ordinary interior-disc values.
(iv) With these choices, the character formula for every KC is the positive real Euler-product constant of Theorem 3.42, and the scalarnormalised product vector is Fourier-equivalent to the non-trivial fixedlabel Perron vector.
Proof. Item (i) is exactly the Hermitian class-indicator normalisation of
Lemma 3.60. Item (ii) is Lemma 3.62. Item (iii) combines the scalar-triple
deletion rule of Lemma 3.63 with the determinant evaluation and branch
statement in Proposition 3.64. The real positive branch in item (iv) is
Lemma 3.61, while the Fourier equivalence is Proposition 3.55 and theorems 3.47 and 3.73. Hence all convention choices used by Main Theorem III
are consequences of previously proved finite-state statements and cannot
be varied independently. Conversely, equality of the two positive productconstant formulas is equality of their real logarithms; subtracting the scalar
normalisation yields the same fixed-label coordinate identity. Thus the compatibility statement fixes both the sign and the global |C|/|G| factor used
in Main Theorem III, rather than adding an independent normalisation
choice.
□

The canonicity assertion is immediate from this inclusion. Any finite

table, Perron root, branch tags, and tail bound is a finite truncation of
the same absolutely convergent determinant series (101). Each verified disc

intersect in that value, regardless of harmless choices in interval subdivision
or in the order of finite summation.
It remains only to justify the stated finite-data format. For fixed M , the
set of triples (r, m, π) with rm ≤ M is finite, all Adams coefficients in it are
computed by finite character-table sums, and each determinant logarithm
is evaluated on the branch fixed in item (c). Replacing each such logarithm
by an interval of radius ϵ0 changes the weighted finite sum by at most the
triangle-inequality bound in (108). Choosing ϵ0 so that this finite bound
is at most the remaining budget after the rational tail radius in (103), and
choosing M by that rational inequality, gives a rigorous enclosure interval of
radius at most ϵ. Thus the input, branch convention, finite enclosure, and
error bound are all finite and explicit.

Boundary determinant convention for Main Theorem III. The scalar Perron
triple (r, m, π) = (1, 1, 1) is deleted before any boundary evaluation in the
fixed-label coordinate formula; the remaining rm = 1 terms are non-trivial
Abel-boundary values, and every rm ≥ 2 term is an ordinary interior-disc
logarithm. Thus no product formula evaluates Log det(I −λ−1 A1 )−1 , assigns
it a finite part, or recovers it by cancellation after the Abel limit.

Corollary 3.68 (Finite branch–tail verification of the product vector).
Fix the determinant model, character table, class-power maps, Perron root,
twisted gap verification, deleted triple convention, branch tags, and rational

FINITE-GROUP SFT EXTENSIONS AND PRODUCT CONSTANTS

95

tail and finite-propagation radii used for a Main Theorem III finite enclosure. If these quantities satisfy the four checks in Proposition 3.67 for every
non-trivial ϱ ∈ Irr(G), then the resulting product-constant vector is uniquely
determined by the displayed finite verification: it is obtained by evaluating
the verified fixed-label discs for Fϱ (λ−1 ), applying the Hermitian Fourier
formula (110), and using the positive real branch fixed in Lemma 3.61.
In particular the finite enclosure contains no hidden determinant branch
choice, no finite value assigned to Log det(I − λ−1 A1 )−1 , and no unrecorded
limiting primitive-orbit enumeration.
Proof. Apply Proposition 3.67 separately to each non-trivial irreducible representation. The deleted-triple check removes the only singular scalar Perron logarithm before any boundary value is evaluated. The branch-tag check
identifies every remaining boundary logarithm with the Abel branch normalized from the germ at zero, and identifies every rm ≥ 2 logarithm with the
ordinary value inside the Perron disc. The tail and finite-propagation checks
give, for each ϱ ̸= 1, a finite rational disc containing the single value Fϱ (λ−1 );
any two finite enclosures satisfying the same checks have discs meeting at
that value by the final assertion of Proposition 3.67.
The product constants are then only a finite change of coordinates. Indeed Corollary 3.69 and (110) express the normalized logarithms of the positive constants (KC )C as the Hermitian character-table Fourier transform
of the vector (Fϱ (λ−1 ))ϱ̸=1 , together with the fixed scalar normalization
C(A). Since the character table, class-power maps, scalar normalization,
and positive real branch have all been fixed in the finite enclosure data or
in the preceding lemmas, this finite transform introduces no new analytic
choice. Thus the branch–tail enclosure determines exactly the determinantcoordinate constants asserted in Main Theorem III.
□

Proof. This is the finite-tail stability statement used for the Perron-point
and product constants; it is not a secondary-term asymptotic theorem. Any
refinement that tries to identify secondary edges, oscillatory terms, edgeisolation hypotheses, product-tail refinements, or dominant secondary moduli requires hypotheses beyond this proposition.
The assertion is a limit comparison at the Perron-boundary scale. The
formulas (116) and (117) are exactly the class-Mertens and class-product formulas already proved in Theorems 3.33 and 3.42. In the refined summatory
identity (115) the lower-order contribution is the single compensated tail
−TC (N ). Since the hypotheses require TC (N ) → 0, passing to the constant
term in the normalized partial sums leaves MC (A, τ ; G) unchanged.
The Euler-product proof of Theorem 3.42 depends on the counting data
only through this constant term and through the absolutely convergent r ≥ 2
primitive tail. Replacing the pointwise count by (114) changes the normalized logarithm only by the same vanishing tail and by a remainder which is
already absorbed in the displayed o(1) term. Therefore the limiting positive
product constant is still the value in (117).
Finally, Proposition 3.64 determines each non-trivial value Fϱ (λ−1 ) from
the finite determinant data and the fixed Abel branch independently of any
lower-order comparison term. The inverse Fourier recovery of Theorem 3.47
then recovers the same fixed-label vector from the normalized product constants. Thus any two refinements satisfying the tail condition have identical
main Mertens constants, product constants, and recovered fixed-label Perron values.

theorems.
3.5.10. Artin-obstruction comparison and Parseval lower bounds. This subsection studies the obstruction attached to the product constants of Main
Theorem III. The question is whether a branch-compatible replacement for
the fixed-label vector can give the same class products. The finite S3 rational
windows later in the paper provide exact witnesses to this obstruction.

Remark 3.75 (Obstruction refinements). Under the hypotheses of Theorem 3.42, the core Main Theorem III data consist of the Frobenius-class
product asymptotic, the fixed-label constants KC , the finite determinant
evaluation of Fϱ (λ−1 ), and the Adams–Möbius obstruction to the naive
Artin substitution. The fixed-coordinate fibre corollary Theorem 3.51 and
the Parseval bound of Corollary 3.74 are determined functorially by those
data together with the already defined periodic Artin logarithms: if two
finite-state extensions have the same scalar baseline γ + log C(A), the same
non-trivial fixed-label boundary values Fϱ (λ−1 ), and the same periodic
Artin logarithms Lϱ (λ−1 ), then they have the same scalar-normalised fixedcoordinate product vector in the branch-compatible determinant chart, the
same class constants, and the same Artin-substitution obstruction bound.
Conversely, equality of the scalar-normalised fixed-coordinate product
vector in that fixed chart forces equality of all non-trivial fixed-label boundary values. Hence the fixed-coordinate fibre corollary and the Parseval
obstruction bound are corollary-level refinements of Main Theorem III.
Main Theorem III, in the form of Theorem 3.42, gives the constants

The same corollary gives the converse inside that chart: equality of scalar-normalised fixedcoordinate product vectors implies equality of their non-trivial Peter–Weyl
coordinates, hence equality of all values Fϱ (λ−1 ).
The Artin-substitution constants are obtained from the same displayed
formula after replacing each fixed-label value by Lϱ (λ−1 ). Therefore the
difference vector Dϱ = Fϱ (λ−1 ) − Lϱ (λ−1 ), the zero-mean classwise error

logarithms. If the periodic Artin logarithms also agree, then the difference
vector agrees, and so the Parseval bound agrees. No additional asymptotic
input, determinant channel, or Perron-point hypothesis is introduced.

Remark 3.78 (On the twisted spectral-gap hypothesis). The condition
η = max ρ(Aϱ ) < λ
ϱ̸=1

does not enter the determinant identities themselves: the formulas of Theorem 3.7 and corollaries 3.10 and 3.18 are valid throughout the disc |z| < λ−1
without it. The role of the hypothesis is exactly to permit evaluation at the
Perron point z = λ−1 in the non-trivial channels and to convert the primitive Peter–Weyl series into the class-indexed constants MC (A, τ ; G). From
the dynamical point of view this is the representation-by-representation analogue of separating the Perron eigenvalue from the remaining spectrum.
The leading Chebotarev and Frobenius-class Mertens phenomena for finite homogeneous extensions belong to the existing orbit-counting lineage,
beginning here with Noorani–Parry [27] and Noorani’s Markov-shift extension criteria [26], and continuing through the Axiom A, Livšic, skew-product
reciprocity, and homology-class settings of [31, 30, 33, 34, 36, 18, 42, 35, 7].
The present paper uses the gap as a stated finite-dimensional hypothesis,
not as a new structural criterion for cocycles. In the finite-state setting it
is already checkable: Lemma 3.4 identifies it with the absence of non-trivial
e equivalently with A
e
peripheral spectrum in the skew-product adjacency A,
e
having a simple strictly dominant Perron eigenvalue, and, when A is irree A structural criterion on τ implying this
ducible, with primitivity of A.
condition without first testing the full skew-product matrix would be a useful further result. The contribution here is the finite-state Adams–Möbius
calculus at the level of primitive constants and the explicit class-indexed determinant formula MC (A, τ ; G), after the leading-density theory of [20, 8].
3.6. Hermitian Fourier readout of class constants. The class-indexed
constants MC (A, τ ; G) form the readout interface between Main Theorems II and III. Main Theorem II first produces their deviations from the
trivial average as Perron-point primitive channels, using the Adams–Möbius
determinant formula and the strict twisted gap. The present subsection
then applies Hermitian character orthogonality to read those channels back
from the class constants. This is the final step in Main Theorem II and
the vector of Perron-point values that Main Theorem III inserts into the
fixed-label Euler product; it is not a cyclic one-layer sampling argument.

Together with Theorem 3.33 and Proposition 3.77, this proves Main Theorem II from the introduction. The determinant and Adams–Möbius work
has already produced the Perron-point primitive channels; the final step
above is standard character-orthogonality recovery on class functions, not
an import of additional cyclic-lift data.
Remark 3.80 (recovery analogy stops at the orthogonality step). Theorem 3.79 has the same formal linear-algebra shape as ordinary Fourier
inversion, but it is used here only on the finite character table of the
fixed group G. In the scalar cyclic-lift comparison case, root-of-unity
layers belong to the single matrix A, not to the finite-group class profile.
Here the full family of conjugacy-class Mertens constants MC (A, τ ; G)
recovers the non-trivial twisted primitive values Πϱ (λ−1 ) by non-abelian
character orthogonality. The analogy is only at the final recovery step: the
class-indexed constants being inverted here have first been produced by the
Adams–Möbius determinant mechanism and the twisted gap, whereas those
fixed-matrix calculations are not part of the finite-group theorem chain
proved in this article.
4. Finite-state criteria for the twisted spectral gap
The strict inequality η = maxϱ̸=1 ρ(Aϱ ) < λ is the finite-state hypothesis
that permits the non-trivial Peter–Weyl channels in Main Theorem II, and
hence the fixed-label Euler coordinates in Main Theorem III, to be evaluated
at the Perron point; if G is trivial, this empty maximum is 0. This section
gives finite-state criteria for that hypothesis and describes the equality case
η = λ. The equality case is rigid in the precise sense needed here: a nontrivial channel has Perron spectral radius exactly when the cocycle admits
an equivariant line field. The resulting loop-character criteria are used as
finite checks for the Perron-point hypotheses in Main Theorems II–III.

Corollary 4.2 (Finite-state criterion for the strict twisted gap). The strictgap hypothesis used in Main Theorems II–III is equivalent, for the fixed
finite extension, to the absence of the following Perron-boundary line fields.
Suppose G is non-trivial. Then the following are equivalent.
(1) The Perron-point input used in the class-product theorem fails,
namely η = λ.
(2) There exist a non-trivial irreducible representation ϱ ∈ Irr(G), a
phase ω ∈ T, and unit vectors ξi ∈ Vϱ , one for each vertex, such
that
(136)

ϱ(τ (e))ξj = ωξi

for every admissible edge e : i → j.

Consequently, for this fixed finite-state cocycle, η < λ holds exactly when
the finite list of edge equations (136) has no solution in any non-trivial irreducible representation. In this case the hypotheses of Theorem 3.42 are
reduced to the verified absence of these boundary line fields.

FINITE-GROUP SFT EXTENSIONS AND PRODUCT CONSTANTS

111

Proof. By Proposition 3.5, every non-trivial channel satisfies ρ(Aϱ ) ≤ λ.
Since G is finite, there are only finitely many irreducible representations, so
the maximum defining η is achieved. Hence η = λ if and only if ρ(Aϱ ) = λ
for at least one non-trivial ϱ ∈ Irr(G). Applying Theorem 4.1 to that representation gives exactly the edge equations (136), and the converse implication in the same theorem shows that any solution of those equations forces
ρ(Aϱ ) = λ, hence η = λ. The final sentence is the contrapositive, together
with the role of the strict-gap hypothesis stated in Theorem 3.42.
□
Corollary 4.3 (Loop-character criterion for boundary channels). Fix a nontrivial irreducible unitary representation ϱ : G → U (Vϱ ). For a phase ω ∈ T
and a vertex i, let
Wi (ϱ, ω) :=

ρ(Aϱ ) = λ if and only if there are a phase ω ∈ T and unit vectors ξi ∈
Wi (ϱ, ω), one for each vertex, such that
ϱ(τ (e))ξj = ωξi

(e : i → j).

For every such solution, the line Li := Cξi is a one-dimensional Γi (τ )subrepresentation of ϱ|Γi (τ ) , with character χi : Γi (τ ) → T determined by
χi (τ (γ)) = ω |γ|

(γ : i → i).

Moreover, if p : i → j is any admissible path of length |p|, then ω −|p| ϱ(τ (p))
gives a unitary similarity Lj → Li . Thus, for the fixed cocycle under consideration, the Perron-boundary obstruction is detected by a compatible system
of one-dimensional loop characters and edge similarities.
Proof. If ρ(Aϱ ) = λ, then Theorem 4.1 supplies ω and unit vectors ξi satisfying the displayed edge equations. Iterating those equations around a closed
path γ : i → i gives
ϱ(τ (γ))ξi = ω |γ| ξi ,
so ξi ∈ Wi (ϱ, ω) for every i. Conversely, if such unit vectors in the spaces
Wi (ϱ, ω) satisfy the edge equations, the converse half of Theorem 4.1 gives
ρ(Aϱ ) = λ.
It remains only to identify the character data. The closed-path identity
just displayed says that every generator τ (γ) of Γi (τ ) preserves Li and acts
on it by the scalar ω |γ| . Products and inverses of such generators therefore
also preserve Li , and the scalar by which an element of Γi (τ ) acts on the
one-dimensional space Li defines a well-defined group homomorphism χi :
Γi (τ ) → T. This homomorphism has the stated value on every closed-path
label. Finally, iterating the edge equations along a path p : i → j gives
ϱ(τ (p))ξj = ω |p| ξi ; hence ω −|p| ϱ(τ (p)) maps the unit line Lj unitarily onto
Li . This proves the announced boundary criterion.
□
Proposition 4.4 (Loop-subgroup obstruction to Perron-boundary channels). Suppose G is non-trivial, and fix a vertex i0 . Put H := Γi0 (τ ) and

H ′ := [H, H]. If the Perron-boundary obstruction η = λ occurs, then there
is a non-trivial irreducible representation ϱ ∈ Irr(G) such that
′

(Vϱ )H := {v ∈ Vϱ : ϱ(h)v = v for every h ∈ H ′ }
is non-zero. Equivalently, if for some vertex i0 every non-trivial irreducible
representation ϱ of G satisfies (Vϱ )[Γi0 (τ ),Γi0 (τ )] = 0, then η < λ. In that case
the strict twisted spectral-gap hypothesis required in Main Theorems II–III,
and hence in Theorems 3.33 and 3.42, is certified by this single loop-subgroup
test.
Proof. Assume first that η = λ. By Corollary 4.2, there are a non-trivial
irreducible representation ϱ, a phase ω ∈ T, and a boundary line field (ξi )i .
Applying Corollary 4.3 at the fixed vertex i0 , the line Li0 := Cξi0 is invariant
under H = Γi0 (τ ), and the action of H on that line is by a one-dimensional
character χ : H → T. Since T is abelian, χ is trivial on the commutator
subgroup H ′ = [H, H]. Hence every element of H ′ fixes the non-zero vector
′
ξi0 , so ξi0 ∈ (Vϱ )H and the displayed fixed-vector space is non-zero.
The equivalent obstruction statement is the contrapositive of the first
assertion, together with the finite-state boundary criterion Corollary 4.2:
if the fixed-vector space above vanishes for every non-trivial irreducible ϱ,
then the boundary case η = λ cannot occur. Since Proposition 3.5 gives
ρ(Aϱ ) ≤ λ in every non-trivial channel and the maximum defining η is finite,
exclusion of equality is exactly η < λ. The final sentence records the role of
this certified strict gap in the two Perron-point product theorems cited in
the statement.
□
Corollary 4.5 (Perfect loop groups force the eta gap). Suppose G is nontrivial. Assume that for some vertex i0 the loop-label subgroup Γi0 (τ ) is
perfect and that, for every non-trivial irreducible representation ϱ of G, the
restricted representation ϱ|Γi0 (τ ) has no non-zero fixed vector. Then η < λ.
In particular, if Γi0 (τ ) = G for some vertex i0 and G is perfect, then the
strict eta-gap hypothesis required in Main Theorems II–III holds.
Proof. Let H = Γi0 (τ ). Since H is perfect, [H, H] = H. The fixed-vector
hypothesis is therefore precisely the vanishing condition in Proposition 4.4,
and that proposition gives η < λ.
For the stated special case, let Γi0 (τ ) = G and assume that G is perfect.
If a non-trivial irreducible ϱ had a non-zero G-fixed vector, the span of that
vector would be a non-zero invariant subspace on which G acts trivially;
irreducibility would force ϱ itself to be the trivial representation. This is
impossible for ϱ ̸= 1. Thus the fixed-vector hypothesis in the first part is
automatic, and the strict gap follows.
□
Proposition 4.6 (Finite-state certificate for the Perron-point hypothesis).
Assume λ > 1. Let Ae be the skew-product adjacency matrix attached to τ ,
and set
e
χAe(t) := det(tI − A).
The following finite-state conditions are equivalent.

FINITE-GROUP SFT EXTENSIONS AND PRODUCT CONSTANTS

113

(i) The strict twisted spectral gap used in the class-Mertens and
Frobenius-class product theorems holds:
η = max ρ(Aϱ ) < λ.
ϱ̸=1

(ii) The skew-product characteristic polynomial has exactly one root on
the Perron circle, namely a simple root at λ; equivalently χAe(t) =
(t − λ)q(t) with every root of the finite polynomial q having modulus
strictly less than λ.
(iii) No non-trivial irreducible representation admits a boundary line field:
for every ϱ ̸= 1, the finite algebraic system
ϱ(τ (e))ξj = ωξi

(e : i → j),

(i ∈ V )

has no solution.
(iv) The deterministic Perron-circle test returns Pass. This test computes
χAe, removes the distinguished factor t − λ, isolates the finitely many
remaining algebraic roots in disjoint rational boxes, and verifies by
exact algebraic comparison that every remaining root has modulus
strictly smaller than λ. If one of these comparisons fails, the test
returns Fail and the failed root box identifies a non-trivial boundary
block through the Peter–Weyl decomposition.
Consequently the Perron-point assumptions in Theorems 3.33 and 3.42 are
exactly λ > 1 plus any one of the finite certificates above. The pass/fail
output is deterministic: a pass certifies the simultaneous availability of all
non-trivial Perron-point determinant branches, while a fail is equivalent to
the existence of a non-trivial boundary line field and hence to a Perron-circle
pole in some non-trivial determinant factor. The loop-subgroup obstruction
of Proposition 4.4 gives a one-vertex finite obstruction certificate for item
(i). In particular, the perfect-loop criterion of Corollary 4.5 is a route into
the same finite-state certificate, not an additional product-constant formula.
Under these certificates the constants MC (A, τ ; G) and KC are those supplied by Theorems 3.33 and 3.42; if the equivalent conditions fail, this paper
asserts no Perron-point evaluation for the non-trivial primitive channels.
Proof. The equivalence of (i) and (ii) is exactly the skew-product peripheralspectrum criterion of Lemma 3.4: the Peter–Weyl block decomposition of Ae
e with algebraic multiplicities, as the disjoint union
gives the spectrum of A,
of the spectra of the blocks Aϱ , each repeated dim ϱ times. The trivial block
is A, and primitivity of A makes λ the only peripheral eigenvalue of that
block and makes it simple. Thus all remaining roots of χAe lie inside |t| < λ
precisely when every non-trivial block has spectral radius strictly smaller
than λ.
The equivalence of (i) and (iii) is Corollary 4.2 when non-trivial irreducible
representations exist. If none exist, then G is trivial, η = 0 by convention,
and the finite line-field condition is vacuous, so both (i) and (iii) hold because
λ > 1. In the remaining case, Theorem 4.1 says that equality ρ(Aϱ ) = λ for a
fixed non-trivial irreducible representation is equivalent to the displayed linefield equations for that representation. Since G is finite, only finitely many
irreducible representations occur, and the maximum defining η is attained.

114

HAOBO MA

Therefore the strict gap fails exactly when one of these finite systems has a
solution.
Item (iv) is the same criterion written as a finite deterministic test. The
matrices are finite, hence χAe and the quotient q = χAe/(t − λ) are finite
algebraic data. A finite root-isolation record for q consists of disjoint rational boxes, each carrying its root multiplicity, together with exact sign
comparisons for |α|2 < λ2 . Such comparisons are finite algebraic comparisons. Therefore the test returns Pass exactly when item (ii) holds. If it
returns Fail, item (ii) fails; by the already proved equivalence with (i) and
(iii), the failed peripheral root lies in a non-trivial Peter–Weyl block and is
represented by a boundary line field.
The final assertion is a substitution of this certificate into the hypotheses
of Theorems 3.33 and 3.42. Those theorems require λ > 1 and η < λ for the
Perron-point evaluation of the non-trivial primitive Peter–Weyl channels;
the present proposition only identifies that hypothesis with finite matrix or
finite algebraic tests. The cited loop-subgroup obstruction and perfect-loop
consequence are retained here only as fixed-cocycle boundary diagnostics for
that test. None of these boundary criteria supplies an additional constant
formula when the tests fail, and no general classification of cocycles forcing
η < λ is used below.
□
Proposition 4.7 (Perron-point separation argument for Main Theorems
II–III). Assume λ > 1. The strict twisted spectral gap η < λ is equivalent to
each of the following Perron-point analytic conditions.
(i) For every non-trivial irreducible representation ϱ, the branch
log det(I − zAϱ )−1 =

n

ϱ )z
n≥1

used in Main Theorem I extends holomorphically to a neighbourhood
of the closed disc |z| ≤ λ−1 .
(ii) For every non-trivial class function ϕ with zero average on G, all
non-trivial Peter–Weyl determinant coordinates entering the Adams–
Möbius formula for Πϕ have branch-compatible values at z = λ−1 , and
hence the Abel-path Perron values used in Theorems 3.33 and 3.42 are
computed entirely inside the finite determinant data.
If the strict gap fails, then there are a non-trivial irreducible representation
ϱ, a phase ω ∈ T, and a boundary line field satisfying (134). Moreover
ωλ ∈ spec(Aϱ ), so the non-trivial determinant factor det(I − zAϱ )−1 has a
pole at z = (ωλ)−1 on the Perron circle. Thus the finite-state certificate of
Proposition 4.6 is a necessary and sufficient verification gate for the Perronpoint constants in Main Theorems II–III; it is not an automatic existence
theorem for arbitrary cocycles.
Proof. The equivalence between η < λ and item (i) is purely finite dimensional. If η < λ, then every eigenvalue α of every non-trivial block Aϱ
satisfies |α| ≤ ρ(Aϱ ) < λ. Hence 1/α, for α ̸= 0, lies strictly outside the
Q
closed disc |z| ≤ λ−1 . The determinant det(I − zAϱ ) = α (1 − zα) therefore has no zero on a neighbourhood of that closed disc, and the logarithm
branch normalized at 0 extends holomorphically there.

FINITE-GROUP SFT EXTENSIONS AND PRODUCT CONSTANTS

115

Conversely, if item (i) holds and some non-trivial Aϱ had ρ(Aϱ ) = λ, then
an eigenvalue α ∈ spec(Aϱ ) would satisfy |α| = λ. The determinant det(I −
zAϱ ) would vanish at z = α−1 , a point of the Perron circle, so the reciprocal
determinant and its normalized logarithm could not be holomorphic in any
neighbourhood of the closed Perron disc. This contradicts item (i). Since
Proposition 3.5 gives ρ(Aϱ ) ≤ λ for every ϱ, item (i) forces η < λ.
Item (ii) is exactly the same condition after finite character decomposition.
The zero-average class functions have no trivial character coordinate. Their
periodic logarithms are finite linear combinations of the non-trivial determinant logarithms by Theorem 3.7, and the primitive series are then obtained
from those finitely many logarithms by the Adams–Möbius formula of Corollary 3.10. Therefore the Perron values used by Theorems 3.33 and 3.42 are
available from the determinant data precisely when the non-trivial determinant branches in item (i) extend through the closed Perron disc. The
trivial channel is deliberately excluded here; it is singular at z = λ−1 and is
handled separately by the scalar finite-part normalisation.
Finally suppose the strict gap fails. Because only finitely many irreducibles occur and each non-trivial block has spectral radius at most λ,
there is a non-trivial ϱ with ρ(Aϱ ) = λ. Theorem 4.1 supplies a phase ω
and a boundary line field satisfying (134); the converse half of its proof constructs an eigenvector of the column realization with eigenvalue ωλ. The
row and column realizations have the same spectrum, so ωλ ∈ spec(Aϱ ).
Hence det(I − zAϱ ) = 0 at z = (ωλ)−1 , and the reciprocal determinant
has a boundary pole. This is precisely the obstruction detected by Proposition 4.6.
□
Corollary 4.8 (Line fields are the only Perron-boundary obstruction). Under the standing hypotheses with λ > 1, the results of this section export
exactly the following finite-state alternative to the main theorem chain:
(i) either no non-trivial boundary line field exists, equivalently η < λ, and
the Perron-point hypotheses of Theorems 3.33 and 3.42 are verified;
(ii) or a non-trivial boundary line field exists, equivalently η = λ, and some
non-trivial determinant factor has a pole on the Perron circle, marking
failure of the Perron-point gate for the class-Mertens and class-product
constants.
In particular, the loop-character and loop-subgroup statements in this section
are criterion-level consequences of the boundary alternative for the fixed finite
input, and the stated hypotheses give strict-gap criteria exactly when they
eliminate every possible boundary line field.
Proof. Corollary 4.2 proves that the absence of non-trivial boundary line
fields is equivalent to η < λ, while the existence of such a line field is equivalent to η = λ. The first alternative is exactly the strict-gap hypothesis
invoked in Theorems 3.33 and 3.42. For the second alternative, Proposition 4.7 shows that the same line field produces a non-trivial eigenvalue on
the Perron circle and hence a boundary pole in the corresponding determinant factor. Therefore the non-trivial Perron branches used by the main
product theorem are available precisely in the first alternative. The loopcharacter classifier and the loop-subgroup obstruction are proved by starting

116

HAOBO MA

from such a boundary line field and recording necessary consequences, or by
imposing explicit hypotheses that rule out all such fields.
□
Corollary 4.9 (Perron-point availability for the main constants). Assume
G is non-trivial. For the fixed finite edge cocycle of this paper, the eta-gap
section supplies Main Theorems II–III with the following boundary interface:
(i) The finite-state criterion of Proposition 4.6 is a deterministic pass/fail
test for the fixed cocycle. It succeeds if and only if the strict-gap hypothesis η < λ is available and if and only if all non-trivial determinant
logarithms have the Perron-point branches certified by Proposition 4.7.
(ii) On this successful branch, every non-trivial fixed-label value Fϱ (λ−1 ),
every class-Mertens constant MC (A, τ ; G), and every product constant
KC is the value already prescribed by the Adams–Möbius determinant
formulas, the fixed-label Euler transform, and class-function Fourier
inversion. Thus the eta-gap section supplies the availability of the
Perron-point evaluation for the already fixed determinant branches.
(iii) On the failed branch, a non-trivial boundary line field exists and a nontrivial determinant factor has a pole on the Perron circle. Hence the
branch-compatible Perron-point vector needed for Main Theorems II–
III is absent, while Main Theorem I remains unchanged because it
uses open-disc determinant identities. The failed branch is therefore a
boundary obstruction for the fixed cocycle.
The explicit line-field exclusions proved in this section are the finite-state
checks for this Perron-point gate.
Proof. Proposition 4.6 proves that passing the operational certificate is exactly η < λ. The equivalence with the existence of the required Perron-point
determinant branches is Proposition 4.7. This proves item (i).
Under these equivalent conditions, the role of the eta-gap certificate in
the article is the role stated in Theorems 3.33 and 3.42: it permits evaluation of the non-trivial primitive Peter–Weyl channels at z = λ−1 . The
values of those channels are then fixed by the finite determinant formulas
of Proposition 3.64 and corollary 3.45, inserted into the Fourier formulas of
Proposition 3.77 and theorem 3.79, and finally inserted into the fixed-label
Euler transform of Theorem 3.42 and proposition 3.40. Each cited formula
is a function of the finite twisted determinant data and the scalar normalisation. Therefore success of the eta-gap certificate supplies the permission
to evaluate the already fixed determinant branches. This proves item (ii).
If the certificate fails, then Proposition 4.6 and theorem 4.1 give a nontrivial boundary line field. By Proposition 4.7, the same line field produces a
pole of a non-trivial determinant factor on the Perron circle, so the simultaneous branch-compatible Perron-point vector required by Main Theorems II–
III is absent. The open-disc determinant identities of Main Theorem I do
not evaluate at λ−1 , so they are unaffected. This proves item (iii). The final
sentence follows from Corollary 4.8, which records the finite alternative and
its explicit line-field exclusions.
□
Remark 4.10 (Strict-gap criteria boundary). The criteria above characterize,
for a fixed finite cocycle, the boundary η = λ by a finite line-field test and provide the hypothesis check needed for Main Theorems II–III. They are finite

FINITE-GROUP SFT EXTENSIONS AND PRODUCT CONSTANTS

117

criteria for the present fixed datum, not a general gap-forcing theory for variable cocycles. The loop-character classifier Corollary 4.3 and perfect-loopgroup consequence Corollary 4.5 record the structural consequences of this
boundary test that are used in the finite-determinant product-constant theorem chain. No broad graph-theoretic or representation-theoretic sufficientcondition package for η < λ is asserted here.
5. Quotient shadows and cyclic-detection boundary
This section is a support section for the Adams obstruction used at the
end of Main Theorem III. After the constants of Main Theorems II–III have
already been constructed, quotient functoriality identifies the abelianised
primitive profile, and Peter–Weyl orthogonality locates the part that no
cyclic quotient can see. The final S3 model then exhibits the non-abelian
defect by exact rational windows.
The section is deliberately not a quotient-reconstruction or cyclicdetection paper. Cyclic quotients of the fixed cocycle are kept separate
from the canonical cyclic-lift root-of-unity layer used later in Section 6;
neither construction is treated as data varying over graph resolutions
or folds. The only role of these boundary results is to show where the
fixed-label Euler vector, and hence the Adams obstruction, escapes abelian
or cyclic shadow data. Throughout this section, “boundary” means the
exact linear-algebraic boundary of what cyclic quotients can detect inside
the class-function space of G.
5.1. Quotient functoriality and the abelian shadow.

FINITE-GROUP SFT EXTENSIONS AND PRODUCT CONSTANTS

vector, this expansion is orthogonal to 1G . Hence ⟨BA,τ ;G , 1G ⟩G = 0, and
the two asserted projection statements follow.
For two cocycles over the same A, the scalar term M(A)1G is identical
in the two decompositions. Subtracting the two copies of (138) and using
(137) for each cocycle gives (139). Orthogonality of the irreducible character
basis then gives the stated if and only if: equality of the normalised class
constants is equality of the class-function expansions, hence equality of every
non-trivial Fourier coefficient, and the converse is immediate from the same
expansion.
Finally, the quantities FP(A), M(A), FA , {Ψq (A)}q≥1 , and the root-ofunity layers in Section 6 are functions of the single scalar matrix A. In the
Main Theorem II decomposition just proved, the only term depending solely
on A is M(A)1G . The remaining term is the non-trivial Peter–Weyl expansion (137), whose coefficients are the cocycle-dependent boundary values
Πτϱ (λ−1 ).
It remains to prove the family classification. Since A and G are fixed,
every listed scalar record has the same value for every τ ∈ T. Thus the map
τ 7→ M#
A,τ ;G is constant on T if and only if the right-hand side of
M#
A,τ ;G = M(A)1G + BA,τ ;G
is independent of τ , which is equivalent to BA,τ ;G being independent of τ .
This proves the equivalence of (i) and (ii).
The equivalence of (ii) and (iii) is exactly the uniqueness of Fourier coefficients in the irreducible-character basis applied to (137): equality of the
class functions BA,τ ;G is equivalent to equality of every coefficient Πτϱ (λ−1 )
with ϱ ̸= 1. This proves (140). The final obstruction statement follows by
taking a two-element family T = {τ, τ ′ }. The scalar records agree because
they depend only on A, while the full profiles disagree by the just-proved
equivalence. This proves the claimed boundary for scalar fixed-matrix inputs.
□
Corollary 5.3 (No scalar cyclic-lift descent without a constant class profile). Fix A, G, and a non-empty family T ⊂ Tgap (A, G). Let R(A) be any
collection of fixed-matrix scalar comparison records formed from A, for instance FP(A), M(A), FA , or canonical cyclic-lift scalar root-of-unity layers
attached to A. These records are attached to the single matrix A, with no
fold map, resolution change, spectrum reconstruction claim, or cocycle descent structure included. Then the following are equivalent.
(i) The fixed data R(A), regarded as a candidate record for the family T,
determine a single normalised primitive class profile on T.
(ii) The class-profile map τ 7→ BA,τ ;G is constant on T.
(iii) For every non-trivial ϱ ∈ Irr(G), the Perron-point primitive channel
Πτϱ (λ−1 ) is independent of τ ∈ T.
Consequently, if two gap cocycles over the same matrix A have distinct nontrivial Perron-point primitive channels, then no fixed-matrix scalar or cycliclift comparison record of A descends to a stable cross-cocycle class-profile
invariant on the two-point family they form. Any stronger invariant requires
additional descent data beyond such fixed-matrix scalar records.

120

HAOBO MA

Proof. Since R(A) is formed only from the scalar matrix A, it has the same
value for every τ ∈ T. Thus it can determine at most one candidate class
profile on T. It determines the actual normalised primitive class profile on
the whole family exactly when BA,τ ;G is independent of τ . This proves the
equivalence of (i) and (ii). The equivalence of (ii) and (iii) is the family
classification in Theorem 5.2. The final assertion follows by applying these
equivalences to T = {τ, τ ′ } and using the hypothesis that at least one nontrivial Perron-point primitive channel differs.
□

We now fix the separation between two cyclic operations. First, one may
quotient the fixed cocycle τ : EA → G. If qab : G ↠ Gab is the abelianisation
map and r : Gab ↠ Z/qZ is cyclic, the inherited cocycle r ◦ qab ◦ τ keeps the
original edge labels after reduction modulo q. Its twisted matrices depend
on the matrices of reduced edge-label counts. Second, and independently,
one may change the data by discarding the inherited labels and resetting
the target to Z/qZ with the canonical cyclic-lift cocycle τq (e) = 1 on every
edge, as in Remark 3.19. Only this reset model produces the root-of-unity
determinant channels log det(I − ωqj zA)−1 from the scalar matrix A alone.

5.3. The exact boundary of cyclic detection. The previous theorem
identifies the canonical splitting. The next statement concerns the span obtained by using all cyclic quotient observables together. It says that this
aggregate cyclic information is exactly the one-dimensional sector. An individual cyclic quotient sees only the one-dimensional characters that factor
through that quotient; here we take the span over all cyclic quotients. This
concerns quotient pullbacks on the fixed cocycle. The canonical cyclic lift
of Section 6 instead supplies the scalar root-of-unity comparison model. Enriched non-abelian root-layer records and higher-dimensional sampling theorems are separate shadow questions, not inputs to the determinant chain
below.
For later use, write Ccyc (G) for the linear span of the pullbacks q ∗ f , where
q : G ↠ Z/mZ ranges over all cyclic quotients and f ranges over the class
functions on Z/mZ.
Lemma 5.9 (Cyclic quotients and one-dimensional characters). One has
Ccyc (G) = span{χ ∈ Irr(G) : dim χ = 1}.
Proof. Let q : G ↠ Z/mZ be a cyclic quotient and let f be a class function
on Z/mZ. Since Z/mZ is abelian, its irreducible characters form a basis of
its class functions, so
X

and each ψ ◦ q is a one-dimensional character of G. Hence
Ccyc (G) ⊂ span{χ ∈ Irr(G) : dim χ = 1}.
Conversely, let χ ∈ Irr(G) be one-dimensional. Its image χ(G) ⊂ C× is a
finite subgroup of the multiplicative group of complex numbers, hence cyclic.
Let qχ : G ↠ χ(G) be the induced quotient map, and let ιχ : χ(G) ,→ C×
be the tautological character. Then
χ = qχ∗ ιχ ,
so χ ∈ Ccyc (G). This proves the reverse inclusion.

□

Remark 5.12 (Minimal conjugation-error detector). Proposition 5.11 is precisely the counterexample described in the proof of Proposition 3.2: for
G = C3 , the correct class-indicator formula requires the conjugate (or equivalently the group inverse), and the failure without it sends every class {a} to
the wrong class {a−1 }. In the subsequent S3 example all characters happen
to be real-valued, so this failure does not manifest there; the C3 check is
therefore the minimal test that can detect a conjugation error.
Exit to the finite witness. The quotient and cyclic statements above have
now isolated the only boundary used later: abelian and cyclic shadows see
the one-dimensional sector but do not determine higher-dimensional Peter–
Weyl coordinates. The following S3 witness uses this boundary only to
certify the non-abelian defect in the Adams-corrected product constants; it
does not claim a general quotient reconstruction theorem.

126

HAOBO MA

5.5. The S3 obstruction witness. This section gives the compact S3 obstruction witness promised by the quotient-shadow boundary. Its purpose is
not to develop a certificate theory, but to show that the higher-dimensional
Peter–Weyl defect occurs in a small symbolic skew product and survives at
the product-constant level of Main Theorem III. The main text records only
the model, the certified Π-windows, the certified fixed-label F -windows, and
the resulting Artin-substitution obstruction; matrix arithmetic, primitive coefficient extraction, and rational interval checks are placed in Appendices A
and B. The main text keeps only the rational windows needed for the theorem statement, while the appendices retain the finite rational arithmetic for
checking. No step of Main Theorems I–III depends on those appendix details; they certify the single displayed non-abelian witness. For orientation
only, the appendix check chain is as follows. The finite model, twisted gap,
and vanishing sign Artin logarithm are checked in Proposition A.2; the primitive fixed-label formulae are computed in Proposition A.3; the Perron-point
primitive windows are bounded in Lemma A.5, using the rational logarithm
estimates of Lemma B.1; and the product-level fixed-label windows used in
Main Theorem III are bounded in Lemma B.2.
Lemma 5.13 (Rational windows for the S3 obstruction). In the verified S3
full-shift model below, the only numerical inputs imported from the appendices are the following rational windows:
(i) −342/1000 < Πε (1/2) < −341/1000 and −719/1000 < Πρ (1/2) <
−718/1000, as stated in (168) and proved from the exact rational
truncation-and-tail bounds (180)– (183) in Lemma B.1;
(ii) −381/1000 < Fε (1/2) < −380/1000 and −923/1000 < Fρ (1/2) <
−922/1000, as stated in (188) and (189) and proved in Lemma B.2
by rational logarithm enclosures and rational tail bounds.
These four intervals are exact enough for the main-text obstruction because
the first pair excludes zero in the primitive sign and standard channels,
while the second pair excludes both zero and the sign-channel Artin value
Lε (1/2) = 0. No decimal approximation is used in the proof of the obstruction theorem; every endpoint above is a rational number and every strict
inequality is inherited from the cited appendix lemma.
Proof. The appendix lemma Lemma B.1 proves rational lower and upper
bounds for the finite logarithmic sums and for the remaining positive tails in
the primitive sign and standard channels. The proof of Lemma A.5 imports
precisely those bounds and records the coarser rational windows in (168);
these are item (i).
The appendix lemma Lemma B.2 applies the same rational logarithm
enclosures to the fixed-label Euler-transform sums and records the rational
windows (188) and (189); these are item (ii). Since the displayed intervals
are open rational intervals lying strictly on the negative side of zero, the
primitive windows prove non-vanishing of Πε (1/2) and Πρ (1/2), and the
fixed-label windows prove non-vanishing of Fε (1/2) and Fρ (1/2). Finally
Proposition A.2 proves Lε (z) ≡ 0, so the fixed-label sign window also proves
Fε (1/2) ̸= Lε (1/2). These are exactly the numerical hypotheses used below.
□

FINITE-GROUP SFT EXTENSIONS AND PRODUCT CONSTANTS

127

129

The class functions on S3 have the orthonormal character basis {1, ε, ρ}.
Therefore the mean-zero, non-scalar class-product data have precisely the
two coordinates in (147); there is no further irreducible sector in which an
additional obstruction could live. Since Lε (1/2) = 0 by Theorem 5.14, the
non-zero sign coordinate implies Fε (1/2) ̸= Lε (1/2), and the obstruction
criterion Theorem 3.72 gives a non-zero Artin-substitution error.
Finally, a class function factors through the abelianisation S3 ↠ C2 if and
only if it lies in the span of the two one-dimensional characters 1 and ε. Character orthogonality puts ρ in the orthogonal complement of that span. The
certified inequality Fρ (1/2) ̸= 0 therefore gives a genuine standard-sector
component invisible to all abelian, equivalently cyclic, quotient class observables. Hence the cyclic quotient data do not determine the full productconstant vector.
□
Corollary 5.16 (Product-constant obstruction is already finite-state). In
the certified S3 full-shift model of Theorem 5.14, the obstruction to replacing
the Adams-corrected constants of Main Theorem III by periodic Artin logarithms is completely detected by the two finite rational windows for Fε (1/2)
and Fρ (1/2). In particular, no cyclic quotient recovery can recover the product vector (KC )C⊂S3 : the sign window already forces a non-zero normalized
Artin-substitution error for some class, and the standard window lies in the
non-abelian Peter–Weyl sector orthogonal to every cyclic quotient observable.
Proof. The first assertion is the fixed-label part of Theorem 5.14 together
with the determinant rigidity and obstruction criterion Theorems 3.47
and 3.72: the normalized class-product vector is the Peter–Weyl expansion
whose non-trivial coordinates are the fixed-label values Fϱ (1/2), and
equality with the naive Artin vector would force Fϱ (1/2) = Lϱ (1/2) for
every non-trivial ϱ. The certified sign interval and Lε (1/2) = 0 therefore
give a non-zero discrepancy; Corollary 3.74 converts it into the stated
classwise normalized lower bound. The second assertion follows from Theorems 5.8 and 5.10: cyclic quotient observables see only the one-dimensional
characters of S3 , while the certified non-zero Fρ (1/2) is in the standard
two-dimensional Peter–Weyl sector.
□
6. Scalar normalisation
Throughout this section, A denotes a primitive non-negative real matrix
with Perron root λ > 1. The symbols pn (A) denote the algebraic Möbius
coefficients of (1); they are primitive orbit counts only in the integer multigraph case fixed in Section 2. For the finite-group results, the scalar role
is the trivial-channel finite part M(A) used in Main Theorems II–III. This
section records the finite-part formula and the scalar projection boundary
needed for that normalisation. Fixed-matrix cyclic-lift and root-of-unity
records appear only as comparison records for this scalar layer.

6.2. Scalar interface for the finite-group results. The remaining scalar
material is only a support boundary for the finite-group determinant and
product results. By Theorem 3.47, the non-trivial class-product coordinates
are the fixed-label Peter–Weyl Perron values. The canonical cyclic lift A⟨q⟩ =
A ⊗ Pq from Equation (11) and the root-of-unity layers of FA are used here
only as fixed-matrix scalar comparison records. No spectrum-identifiability,
one-layer reconstruction, or cross-cocycle descent theorem is asserted from
them. The following boundary statement isolates their scalar normalisation
role for Main Theorems II–III.

Remark 6.5 (Fixed-matrix scalar comparison data and the trivial channel).
This is a support statement for Main Theorems II–III. In the setting of
Theorem 6.4, (151) writes the normalised profile as the sum of the constant
class function M(A)1G and the non-trivial Peter–Weyl part BA,τ ;G . Thus
scalar finite-part formulas and the fixed cyclic-lift scalar quantities used here
enter the finite-group theorem chain only as comparison data for the trivialchannel normalisation M(A). They determine no non-trivial class profile on
a family T, except in the degenerate independence case already characterized
in Theorem 6.4.
Verification. Equation (151) is exactly the decomposition of the normalised
class-Mertens vector into its constant class-function component and its zeromean Peter–Weyl component. The proof of Theorem 6.4 identifies the constant component as M(A)1G and the non-trivial component as BA,τ ;G . Every scalar finite-part or fixed cyclic-lift comparison quantity named here depends only on A, hence is constant as τ varies in T. Such scalar comparison
data can therefore determine a non-trivial profile on the whole family precisely when the profiles BA,τ ;G are independent of τ , which is the equivalent
condition already proved in Theorem 6.4.
□
Corollary 6.6 (Scalar comparison is constant on class-profile differences).
Keep the notation and hypotheses of Theorem 6.4. Let T ⊂ Tgap (A, G) be
non-empty, and let
Z(T) := spanR {BA,τ ;G − BA,τ ′ ;G : τ, τ ′ ∈ T}
be the real zero-mean class-function space actually swept out by the nontrivial primitive profiles in the family. Then the scalar finite part FP(A),
the scalar Mertens normalisation M(A), the reduced determinant C(A), and
every fixed-matrix cyclic-lift scalar comparison quantity depending only on
A have rank zero on Z(T): they are constant on T and annihilate all profile
differences.
Consequently, any finite collection of real linear invariants whose restriction to profile differences is linear and which, together with the scalar comparison data, recovers the normalised class-Mertens profile of Main Theorem II

134

HAOBO MA

on T must have real rank at least dim Z(T) on the non-trivial Peter–Weyl
sector.
The same statement holds for the Adams-corrected product constants after
the scalar product normalisation is fixed, with the swept-out class-function
space replaced by the span of the corresponding normalised product-log differences

Proof. The scalar records listed in the statement are functions of the fixed
matrix A. Hence they take the same value at every cocycle in T. On
a difference BA,τ ;G − BA,τ ′ ;G they therefore evaluate to zero, which is the
asserted rank-zero property on Z(T).
Let L1 , . . . , Ls be any real linear invariants as in the statement, and let

If the scalar records together with the invariants recover the normalised

image under L must be equal; otherwise two cocycles with indistinguishable
scalar records and invariant values would have different recovered profiles.
Thus L is injective on Z(T), so its rank is at least dim Z(T).
For products, Theorem 3.47 writes the constants, after the fixed scalar

injectivity argument applied to the span ZK (T) gives the product lower
bound. The final numerical bound is the dimension of the zero-mean classfunction space, namely one less than the number of conjugacy classes. □
The cyclic-lift quantities used here have only the comparison role proved
above: scalar data identify a fixed scalar model, while the non-trivial branchcompatible product coordinates are the fixed-label Peter–Weyl values studied in the finite-group sections. Questions requiring holomorphic scalar variation, finite scalar truncation, spectrum identifiability, or reconstruction from
lift data need additional hypotheses beyond this scalar normalisation. The
product-side finite-state consequence is recorded in Corollary 3.48.
7. Discussion and open problems
7.1. Summary of the main contribution. For a finite-group extension
of a mixing subshift of finite type, the twisted determinant family records
periodic labels. The main point of this paper is that the corresponding
primitive labels are recovered by a finite Adams–Möbius calculation in Peter–
Weyl coordinates. This is the finite-state correction forced by the repetition

FINITE-GROUP SFT EXTENSIONS AND PRODUCT CONSTANTS

135

rule g 7→ g m for primitive orbits. Under the twisted gap η < λ, the recovered
primitive channels have Perron-point values and hence determine the classindexed constants MC (A, τ ; G) in the Frobenius-class Mertens sums.
The Euler-product constants require the same distinction. The classical
Frobenius-class product theorem supplies the existence statement and the
leading exponent |C|/|G|. Once that layer and the scalar normalisation are
fixed, the remaining non-trivial coordinates of the constants are the fixedlabel Euler values Fϱ (λ−1 ), not the periodic Artin logarithms Lϱ (λ−1 ). Thus
the contribution is not a new proof of the Frobenius density exponent, but
a determinant formula for the normalised constant and a rigidity statement
saying that the Adams–Möbius fixed-label vector is the branch-compatible
coordinate vector that produces the true constants. Quotient functoriality
identifies the abelianised primitive profile, and cyclic quotient observables
span precisely the one-dimensional Peter–Weyl shadow. The S3 example
shows both phenomena concretely: the sign line detects an Adams correction invisible to the Artin logarithm, while the standard two-dimensional
representation gives a genuine non-abelian component.
The scalar finite-part and cyclic-lift calculations supply the trivial-channel
normalisation and a fixed-matrix comparison model. The projection theorem marks the corresponding scalar boundary: after normalising the class
constants, scalar data give the constant class-function component M(A)1G ,
whereas the non-trivial primitive profile is the Peter–Weyl vector BA,τ ;G .
7.2. Further directions. Several natural extensions remain beyond the
finite-state determinant setting treated here. One direction is to obtain
secondary-term refinements of the Frobenius-class Mertens formula while
preserving the Adams–Möbius separation between periodic and primitive labels. Another is to give structural criteria for the strict twisted gap η < λ, for
instance in terms of mixing properties of the skew product or representationtheoretic expansion of the cocycle. A third direction is to extend the determinant formula to Hölder/Ruelle transfer operators, where branch choices
and Perron residues would have to be controlled analytically rather than by
finite matrices.
There are also inverse problems suggested by the quotient calculations.
Cyclic quotient observations see only the one-dimensional Peter–Weyl sector, while the canonical cyclic-lift root layer gives a fixed-matrix comparison
model rather than an invariant of the original finite-group cocycle. Reconstruction from restricted observations therefore asks for an observability
theory with explicit descent hypotheses. The rational S3 enclosures in the
appendices exhibit the Adams correction and the failure of the naive Artin
substitution in a concrete finite model.

appendix verifies one finite obstruction witness, not a catalogue or classification of small non-abelian examples. The main text imports only the

Appendix B. Exact rational verification for the S3 example
This appendix gives the finite rational arithmetic for the S3 obstruction
used in Theorem 3.72 and proposition A.4. The main text uses only the
coarse rational non-vanishing windows from Lemma A.5; the pages below
display the exact finite truncations, tail estimates, and interval enclosures
that certify those windows. The material is not a catalogue of finite-group examples and not a general finite-verification theory for arbitrary finite groups.

HAOBO MA

the following finite rational data. Each row records the mathematical input, branch convention, finite truncation, rational tail, and output window;
no floating-point datum or unrecorded limiting enumeration is part of the
certificate.

The first two rows supply the coarse Perron-channel windows used in
Lemma A.5. The last two rows are valid fixed-label enclosures in the sense
of Proposition 3.67 and corollary 3.68 and therefore supply the S3 input
to Theorems 3.42 and 3.73. For every rational logarithm argument q > 1,
the endpoints L3 (q) and U3 (q) are rational and enclose log q by (186).
Multiplying by a positive rational coefficient preserves the endpoint order,
while multiplying by a negative coefficient reverses it. Thus each finite
signed sum in the table has a rational lower endpoint and a rational upper
endpoint obtained by the stated deterministic endpoint-selection rule.
The truncation and tail entries in the first two rows are exactly (181) and
(183); the proof of Lemma B.1 shows that adding those rational radii to the
finite intervals places Πε (1/2) and Πρ (1/2) in the displayed windows. For
the last two rows, Lemma B.2 gives the fixed-label finite sums, the deleted
scalar Perron convention, the listed Adams coefficients, and the rational tails
Bε and Bρ . The denominator-cleared inequalities in that lemma place the
resulting rational intervals in the two fixed-label output windows.
Finally, the fixed-label branch and deletion data in the last two rows are
precisely the legal branch–tail record of Proposition 3.67: the singular scalar
triple is not evaluated, every remaining boundary term has the Abel tag, and
every rm ≥ 2 term is an interior determinant logarithm. Applying Corollary 3.68 then propagates these two verified fixed-label windows through the

□
Finite exact-arithmetic replay. The mathematical verification in this appendix is the displayed rational arithmetic in Lemmas B.1 and B.2. The following finite rational procedure reproduces the four displayed certificate
windows without floating-point input: compute every rational endpoint
L3 (q) ≤ log q ≤ U3 (q) for the rational logarithm arguments occurring in
E7 , R15 , Q8 , and S32 ; select the lower endpoint for each positive signed
coefficient and the upper endpoint for each negative signed coefficient when
forming a lower bound, with the opposite selections for the upper bound;
add the rational tails 1/1105920, 9/524288, Bε , and Bρ ; and clear positive
denominators in the four final comparisons with the printed rational windows. This procedure verifies exactly the inclusions in Corollary B.3. For
every rational argument q > 1, Lemma B.1 proves L3 (q) < log q < U3 (q) by
the positive remainder of the series for 2 arctanh((q − 1)/(q + 1)). Therefore
multiplication by a positive rational coefficient preserves the order of the
interval and multiplication by a negative rational coefficient reverses it. Applying this rule term-by-term to the finite signed sums defining E7 , R15 , Q8 ,
and S32 gives rational lower and upper endpoints for those four finite sums.
The remaining primitive and fixed-label tails are the rational quantities displayed in (181), (183), and in the definitions of Bε and Bρ in Lemma B.2.
Adding these rational tails enlarges the four finite-sum intervals by rational
amounts and hence still gives rational intervals.
The last step is purely algebraic. Each assertion that one of these rational
intervals lies in its printed window is an inequality between rational numbers;
multiplying by the product of the positive denominators is an equivalent
integer comparison. The required positive margins are displayed in the
proofs of Lemmas B.1 and B.2 for Πε (1/2), Πρ (1/2), Fε (1/2), and Fρ (1/2).
Consequently the finite rational procedure recovers exactly the four windows
collected in Corollary B.3.

Appendix C. Appendix: exact rational enclosure data for the
S3 example
This appendix gives the exact rational enclosure data used only for the
local S3 determinant calculation and the Main Theorem III evaluation in
that model. The main text imports just the four numerical inequalities
Πε (1/2) ∈ (−342/1000, −341/1000),

Πρ (1/2) ∈ (−719/1000, −718/1000),

and
Fε (1/2) ∈ (−381/1000, −380/1000),

Fρ (1/2) ∈ (−923/1000, −922/1000),

as recorded in Corollary B.3. The finite algebraic model data, branch-tagged
logarithm enclosures, rational tail bounds, and branch–tail enclosure data
below explain how those inequalities are checked by exact arithmetic. They
are finite interval-enclosure data for this example, not extra analytic input
for Main Theorem III.
Lemma C.1 (Local rational enclosure data for fixed labels). Assume the
hypotheses of Theorem 3.33. Fix a non-trivial ϱ ∈ Irr(G), a tolerance ϵ > 0,
and a truncation level M satisfying (104). The enclosure obtained from
Proposition 3.66 is reproduced by the following finite verification. This verification consists only of finite algebraic model data and rational inequalities
of the following four kinds:
(i) the list of triples (r, m, π) with rm ≤ M , with the scalar Perron triple
(1, 1, 1) deleted exactly as in Lemma 3.63;
(m)
(ii) the Adams coefficients aϱ,π = ⟨ψ m χϱ , χπ ⟩G , written from the finite
character table and class-power table;
(iii) for every listed triple, a branch tag specifying either the Abel non-trivial
Perron branch (rm = 1, π ̸= 1) or the ordinary germ value inside the
Perron disc (rm ≥ 2), together with a rational rectangular interval
enclosure for Log det(I − λ−rm Aπ )−1 ;
(iv) two rational error bounds RM and Rnum : the tail bound RM is verified
either directly from (106) or from the rational envelope (107), and
Rnum is verified from the finite interval-propagation bound (108).
These four kinds of quantities, verified by exact algebraic comparison and
rational interval arithmetic, produce a rational rectangular interval IM ⊂ C
(M )
enclosing the finite sum Fϱ
and verifies the inclusion
(190)

Fϱ (λ−1 ) ∈ IM + {w ∈ C : |w| ≤ RM + Rnum } ,

where all sides of IM and both radii are rational. Thus the displayed finite verification gives an exact rational enclosure for the constant used in
Proposition 3.66 and corollary 3.69.
Proof. The first two entries are finite because the set {(r, m) : rm ≤ M }
and Irr(G) are finite, and because the Adams coefficients are finite charactertable inner products. The deleted scalar triple is exactly the omitted term
in Lemma 3.63 and proposition 3.66; its coefficient is zero for ϱ ̸= 1, so no
finite enclosure assigns a finite value, finite part, or endpoint branch to the
singular scalar logarithm.

151

For each remaining triple, the branch tag is one of the two cases already
proved in item (d) of Proposition 3.66: either the non-trivial Abel branch at
rm = 1, made regular by the strict twisted gap, or the ordinary logarithmic
germ at rm ≥ 2, where ρ(λ−rm Aπ ) < 1. Thus every tagged interval encloses a uniquely specified logarithm. Multiplying the rational rectangular
(m)
intervals by the verified coefficients µ(m)aϱ,π /(rm), enclosing each algebraic
product by exact comparison in a rational rectangle, and summing those
rectangles gives a rational rectangular interval IM which contains the stated
finite truncation. Its excess radius is at most Rnum by the triangle inequality used in (108). The discarded terms with rm > M have total absolute
value at most the rational number RM , either because the enclosure data
compare RM with the algebraic tail in (106), or because it records rational θ, Qϱ satisfying (107) and RM ≥ Qϱ θM +1 /(1 − θ). All these checks are
finite algebraic or rational inequalities. Adding the two independent radii
proves (190). Finally, Corollary 3.69 applies finite Fourier inversion to the
verified fixed-label Perron vector, so the same finite rational data enclose
the product constants without introducing a new analytic premise.
□
Lemma C.2 (Finite branch–tail enclosure format for fixed labels). Under the hypotheses of Theorem 3.42, every finite enclosure produced by the
fixed-label procedure and used for the Main Theorem III product constants is
described, for each non-trivial ϱ ∈ Irr(G), by a branch–tail enclosure record
with the following finite verification entries:
(i) the determinant model (Dπ (z))π∈Irr(G) , the Perron root λ, the twisted
verified gap inequality η < λ, the character table, and the class-power
maps;
(ii) rational tail data Cϱ , Qϱ , θ, M satisfying the majorant hypothesis of
Lemma 3.14, λ−1 < θ < 1, Cϱ λ < Qϱ , and (103);
(iii) the finite window of all triples (r, m, π) with rm ≤ M , with only
(m)
(1, 1, 1) deleted, and all Adams coefficients aϱ,π in that window;
(iv) for every non-deleted listed triple, a branch tag and a rational rectangular enclosure for Log det(I − λ−rm Aπ )−1 , using the Abel tag for
(rm = 1, π ̸= 1) and the ordinary Perron-disc tag for rm ≥ 2;
(v) the rational finite-propagation radius obtained from (108), and the rational tail radius RM,ϱ = Qϱ θM +1 /(1 − θ).
These entries determine a disc
Iϱ + {w ∈ C : |w| ≤ RM,ϱ + Rnum,ϱ }
containing Fϱ (λ−1 ),

where Iϱ is the interval evaluation of the finite sum
(105). Conversely, every finite enclosure produced by Proposition 3.66 is
equivalent, after reordering the finite window and splitting the total error into
its tail and finite-propagation parts, to these entries. This converse concerns
only the stated fixed-label enclosure procedure; it is not a claim that no other
finite enclosure method could prove the same numerical inclusions. Applying
these enclosures for all non-trivial ϱ and then the Fourier formula (110) gives
rational enclosures for all Frobenius-class product constants (KC )C . Thus
the appendix records local finite rational verification data for the displayed
S3 example and not additional analytic hypotheses.

Proof. The model field is finite because the group has finitely many irreducible representations and conjugacy classes, and because each twisted
determinant is a finite polynomial. The tail field is finite and checkable
by algebraic comparison: Cϱ is a verified majorant from Lemma 3.14, the
inequalities for Qϱ and θ are rational/algebraic comparisons, and (103) is
a rational inequality. By Proposition 3.66, this tail field gives the analytic
error bound (106) through the rational envelope (107).
For the finite window, the Adams coefficients are recomputed from the
finite character-table inner product (102). The branch field is exactly the
two-branch convention of Proposition 3.66: the strict twisted gap makes
every non-trivial rm = 1 Abel determinant regular at the boundary, while
rm ≥ 2 lies inside the Perron disc. Signed interval summation over this
finite window gives Iϱ . Enlarging by the finite-propagation radius of (108)
and by RM,ϱ contains the exact value by the triangle inequality and by the
tail estimate already proved. This proves the stated enclosure.
Conversely, the enclosure produced by Proposition 3.66 has no further
analytic datum: it uses the same determinant model, the same Adams coefficients, the same deleted-triple and branch convention, a finite truncation
level, finitely many determinant-logarithm enclosures, and the sum of one
tail error and one finite interval-propagation error. Recording those entries
in the five fields above, and replacing an exact tail comparison by any rational envelope satisfying (103), gives the same verified disc. The converse is
therefore a sufficiency-and-necessity statement for the finite enclosure procedure used here, not a universal optimality assertion about all possible finite
enclosure methods. Finally, Corollary 3.69 expresses the normalized product
logarithms by the finite character-table Fourier transform (110); exponentiating the resulting real intervals gives the stated enclosures for the positive
constants KC .
□
Exact rational interval replay for fixed labels. In the fixed-label rational
enclosure format of Lemmas C.1 and C.2, the truth of a recorded enclosure
for Fϱ (λ−1 ), and hence of the induced enclosure for every Main Theorem III
product constant KC , is decidable by a finite sequence of exact comparisons
in Q and in the finite character table of G. More precisely, the verification
uses no analytic oracle beyond the finite branch–tail fields
Dalg ,

Tϱ (M ),

Lϱ (M ),

Rϱ (M ),

Iϱ (M ),

from Lemma C.2: if those fields pass the branch, Adams-coefficient,
logarithm-interval, tail, and interval-sum checks listed there, then the
resulting rational disc contains the unique value Fϱ (λ−1 ). Conversely,
every enclosure produced by Proposition 3.66 can be written in this finite
enclosure format after replacing its analytic tail inequality by a rational
envelope satisfying (103). The verification begins with the finite algebraic
field Dalg . From the character table and class-power table it recomputes
(m)
each Adams coefficient aϱ,π by the finite inner product (102); this is a finite
sum of algebraic integers, and in the displayed finite-group applications it
is an exact integer comparison. It also checks that the only deleted triple
is (1, 1, 1) and that its coefficient is ⟨χϱ , 1⟩G = 0, so the scalar Perron
logarithm is absent before any boundary value is considered.

For each triple in the finite list Tϱ (M ), one checks the branch tag against
the dichotomy proved in Lemma 3.63 and proposition 3.64: non-trivial
triples with rm = 1 use the Abel boundary branch, while all triples with
rm ≥ 2 use the ordinary interior-disc logarithm. The field Lϱ (M ) supplies
rational rectangular enclosures for those finitely many logarithms. Multi(m)
plying each rectangle by the rational weight µ(m)aϱ,π /(rm) and summing
intervals is finite rational interval arithmetic, so it gives exactly the finitesum interval Iϱ (M ) if and only if all stated interval inclusions hold.
The only remaining issue is the omitted infinite tail. The enclosure data
contains rationals Qϱ and θϱ with λ−1 < θϱ < 1 and Cϱ λ < Qϱ . These are
checked by algebraic or rational comparisons from Dalg . Then, exactly as in

enclosure.
Conversely, inspect the construction in Proposition 3.66. It chooses a
finite cutoff, uses the same Adams coefficients and branch convention, encloses finitely many determinant logarithms, and bounds the remaining tail.
By the density of Q and the strict inequality λ−1 < 1, its tail comparison
can be replaced by rationals satisfying (103). Recording those data in the
same branch–tail fields gives a finite enclosure record with the same verified
disc. Finally, Lemma C.2 transports the fixed-label intervals to the product
constants by finite Fourier inversion and exponentiation on rational real intervals, so the Main Theorem III enclosures are verified by the same finite
rational data.
